# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## dagster-drt

> `dagster-drt` is published as a separate PyPI package with its own version.

### [0.5.0] - 2026-08-31 (dagster-drt)

- **⚠️ Breaking: SQL Server's change sensor now requires `minimum_interval_seconds=`, matching Snowflake's existing requirement** ([#1051](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1051)): both `snowflake.connector.connect()` and `pymssql.connect()` open a fresh, full-auth-handshake connection on every poll — a documented, self-acknowledged asymmetry in this module's own docstring and [ADR 0004](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/blob/main/docs/adr/0004-streaming-and-event-triggered-syncs.md)'s 2026-08-17 amendment, never actually enforced in code until now. **Upgrading:** a SQL Server sensor definition (`build_drt_change_sensor(...)`) that didn't already pass `minimum_interval_seconds=` will start raising `ValueError` at evaluation time — add the argument, there is no compatibility default (matches how Snowflake's own requirement works — the interval is a deliberate choice about your database's tolerance for poll-connection overhead, not something dagster-drt can safely guess). See [`docs/guides/event-driven-syncs.md`](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/blob/main/docs/guides/event-driven-syncs.md) and this package's README for the updated example.

### [0.4.0] - 2026-08-28 (dagster-drt)

- **Chainable `DrtEventIterator` post-processing** ([#182](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/182)): `DagsterDrtResource.run()` now returns an iterator wrapper, matching dagster-dlt's established execution shape, so asset bodies can write `yield from drt.run(context=context).fetch_row_count()`. `fetch_row_count()` independently re-runs each resolved model against the configured **source** and attaches Dagster's standard `dagster/row_count` metadata; it deliberately does not copy `rows_extracted` or `rows_synced`, which remain the engine result from the original run and therefore are not an independent post-load check. The verification query follows the project's `query_tagging` configuration with its own fresh run id, including both the universal SQL comment and native source tags, and incremental models are rendered with the exact `cursor_value_used` by the original sync so they verify the same window. Fixing this surfaced a separate, pre-existing gap in the same code path: `DagsterDrtResource.run()`'s own `run_sync()` call never passed `project.query_tagging` at all, so a project explicitly disabling tagging (`query_tagging.enabled: false`) or setting custom `extra` tags had that config silently ignored for every sync executed through dagster-drt — the CLI's `drt run` path was unaffected, this was dagster-drt-specific. Now fixed alongside the row-count query. Source-count failures are logged and leave the original materialization intact, matching the optional post-processing contract in dagster-dlt. The same wrapper handles both event forms now emitted by the resource — `MaterializeResult` under `@drt_assets` and `AssetMaterialization` under `@op` — without separate chaining APIs.

- **Declarative `DrtSyncComponent`** ([#183](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/183)): drt projects can now be declared in a Dagster `defs.yaml` with `type: dagster_drt.DrtSyncComponent` and `attributes.project_dir`, optional `sync_names`, and `translation.group_name`; the generated definition uses the same `@drt_assets`, translator, and resource execution paths as the Python API. `get_asset_spec()` and `execute()` are public subclass hooks, and the attached scaffolder makes `dg scaffold defs dagster_drt.DrtSyncComponent ...` emit a ready-to-edit YAML definition. The implementation targets Dagster's real current Components API rather than the pre-RC shape from when the issue was filed: `defs.yaml`, top-level Component exports, and the per-project `dg` CLI contract arrived together in **Dagster 1.10.18**, so the package's dependency floor moves from `dagster>=1.6` to `dagster>=1.10.18`. `dagster-dg-cli` remains a project-development tool rather than a dagster-drt runtime dependency, consistent with Dagster's own integration packages. Each component instance's underlying asset op gets a name derived from its declaring `defs.yaml` path (not a fixed literal), so multiple `DrtSyncComponent` declarations in one project merge without a `DagsterInvalidDefinitionError` op-name collision — caught in Codex review on this PR.

- **`@op` execution through `DagsterDrtResource`** ([#184](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/184)): `run()` now accepts both Dagster execution context types. The existing `AssetExecutionContext` path is unchanged — it resolves selected syncs and custom asset keys from `context.assets_def` and yields `MaterializeResult`. The new sibling `OpExecutionContext` path requires explicit, non-empty `sync_names`, resolves those syncs directly without touching asset-definition/spec metadata, derives each `AssetMaterialization.asset_key` through the same default `DagsterDrtTranslator` used by `build_drt_asset_specs()`, and fails clearly on a missing or unknown selection. Dagster 1.12.22 defines `AssetExecutionContext` and `OpExecutionContext` as sibling classes rather than one subclassing the other, so the runtime branch uses the asset-context `isinstance` check Dagster's own dagster-dlt resource uses.

- **`build_drt_change_sensor()` — event-driven activation, Tier 2 of [ADR 0004](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/blob/main/docs/adr/0004-streaming-and-event-triggered-syncs.md)** ([#855](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/855)): a Dagster sensor that polls a cheap, metadata-only change signal on the project's source table — `DeltaTable.version()` for `deltalake` profiles, the catalog's `current_snapshot().snapshot_id` for `iceberg` — and fires one `RunRequest` when it moves. Deliberately not wired to drt's own state: the signal is tracked by Dagster's own sensor cursor (`context.update_cursor()`), a different question from what drt itself has already synced — the triggered run still reads/writes drt's state exactly as before. Both signals are project-wide, not per-sync (a `DeltaLakeProfile`/`IcebergProfile` maps to exactly one physical table, and a project has exactly one profile), so what gets materialized on a fire is entirely up to the `job`/`asset_selection` the caller passes — this function only decides *when*. Only `job=` (a `JobDefinition`) and `asset_selection=` are exposed — not `target=` or multi-job `jobs=`; the former was omitted because this sensor originally shipped against the package's then-`dagster>=1.6` floor, while the latter requires per-`RunRequest` `job_name` disambiguation that one project-wide signal has no principled way to choose. (`DrtSyncComponent` now raises the package floor to 1.10.18, but the sensor's established public surface remains unchanged.) `RunRequest.run_key` is the `previous->current` transition, not the bare destination value — Dagster dedupes run keys globally across every past evaluation, so a bare value would silently drop the run the moment a signal revisits an old one (a source table rollback, or a recreated Delta table restarting its version counter). A permanently-unsupported profile type (anything but `deltalake`/`iceberg`/`snowflake`/`sqlserver` today) raises `NotImplementedError` and surfaces as a failed sensor tick rather than a silent, permanent skip; a transient read failure (network, auth, a catalog hiccup) skips the tick and retries next time. First evaluation with no prior cursor always fires once, matching drt's own "no watermark yet, do a full pass" bootstrap. Snowflake `STREAM` / `SYSTEM$STREAM_HAS_DATA()` — this item's originally-planned Snowflake signal — did **not** ship: it's a boolean that only resets on DML consumption, and drt's read-only Snowflake extraction never consumes it, so a cursor-diff sensor built the same way as Delta/Iceberg would fire once and then latch permanently silent. Snowflake and SQL Server support shipped afterward via a different signal — see the next entry.

- **`build_drt_change_sensor()` — Snowflake and SQL Server support** ([#975](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/975)): closes the gap the entry above left open, via signals verified against real accounts rather than the `STREAM_HAS_DATA()` one that didn't fit. **Snowflake**: `SYSTEM$LAST_CHANGE_COMMIT_TIME('<table>')` — monotonic, side-effect-free, no `CHANGE_TRACKING` prerequisite, no stream/consumption semantics at all. Requires two new keyword-only arguments on `build_drt_change_sensor()`: `watch_table=` (a `SnowflakeProfile` has no single table of its own — it's account/database/schema/warehouse-scoped, unlike `DeltaLakeProfile`/`IcebergProfile`) and `minimum_interval_seconds=` (the call itself is metadata-only and does not touch warehouse compute — verified live against a real account in [#985](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/985), a deliberately-suspended smoke warehouse stayed suspended through the call — but each poll still opens a fresh `snowflake.connector.connect()` session with a full auth handshake, unlike Delta/Iceberg's object-storage metadata reads, so an unbounded poll cadence would repeat that handshake far more than any real source needs, making this a required, deliberate choice). **SQL Server**: `CHANGE_TRACKING_CURRENT_VERSION()` — also monotonic and consumption-free, and requires `watch_table=` too, but only to *validate*: the polled signal itself is database-scoped, not per-table (it fires on any tracked table's change, not just the one a given sync targets — coarser than the other three signals but not unsafe, since an extra sensor-triggered run just finds nothing new to sync), so without checking the specific table, a database with Change Tracking enabled overall but the target table never individually enabled (`ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE CHANGE_TRACKING` is a separate opt-in) would silently never see that table's changes — caught in Codex review on this PR. `watch_table=` is checked against `CHANGE_TRACKING_MIN_VALID_VERSION`, raising `ValueError` if that specific table isn't tracked. Missing/unresolvable-driver imports (`snowflake-connector-python`, `pymssql` — dagster-drt's base install pulls in neither) now also propagate as `ImportError` instead of being caught as a transient skip, a pre-existing gap for the Delta/Iceberg signals too that this PR's Codex review surfaced and fixed for all four. Every NULL-signal and required-argument case raises `ValueError` at evaluation time — propagated the same way `NotImplementedError` already was, not swallowed into a `SkipReason` — for a `NULL` signal (change tracking not enabled, or an unresolvable `watch_table`) rather than coalescing it to a fake baseline, which would silently repeat the exact "fires once, then permanently silent" failure mode this whole issue exists to avoid. See `docs/guides/event-driven-syncs.md` and [ADR 0004](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/blob/main/docs/adr/0004-streaming-and-event-triggered-syncs.md)'s 2026-08-17 amendment.

- **Fixed: `DagsterDrtResource.run()` and the legacy `drt_assets_legacy()` path ignored `state.backend`.** Both constructed `StateManager(project_path)` directly instead of going through [#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756)'s `build_state_bundle()` factory — a project configured with `state.backend: gcs` or `s3` still wrote run state to local disk when executed through dagster-drt, silently defeating the whole point of a remote backend for orchestrator-launched runs. Found while researching [#855](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/855) (dagster-drt sensors): the sensor's premise — a sensor process and a CI-launched `drt run` sharing durable state — doesn't hold if the execution path itself never picks up the remote backend. Also: `integrations/dagster-drt/tests/` (36 tests) had never run in CI (separate fix, `ci.yml` + `publish-dagster-drt.yml`, no version bump).

- **Fixed: `DagsterDrtResource.run()` never passed `history_manager=` to `run_sync()`** ([#980](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/980)) — a Dagster-triggered sync's execution history stayed empty regardless of `state.backend`, since `run_sync()` only appends history when `history_manager` is supplied. The identical gap the drt-core `run_drt_sync()` fix closed for Airflow/Prefect (see drt-core's `[Unreleased]` CHANGELOG entry), found while fixing that one but out of scope for it since `dagster-drt` is a separately-versioned package. Now passes `history_manager=bundle.history` and `history_retention_days=project.history.retention_days`, matching the CLI's own call site.

- Requires `drt-core>=0.9.1`, `dagster>=1.10.18` — the 0.9.0 floor is unsafe for this package: its `StatePersistingObserver` advances the watermark on `DagsterDrtResource.run(dry_run=True)` previews ([#978](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/978), fixed in drt-core 0.9.1)

### [0.3.0] - 2026-04-16 (dagster-drt)

- **Source-volume and incremental-sync observability** ([#343](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/343)) — `DagsterDrtResource.run()` now reports `rows_extracted` and `duration_seconds` metadata, logs the extracted count, and wires watermark storage so Dagster-launched incremental syncs persist and reuse their cursor.
- Requires `drt-core>=0.5.3`, `dagster>=1.6`

### [0.2.0] - 2026-04-04 (dagster-drt)

- **API alignment with dagster-dbt / dagster-dlt patterns** (#176, #177, #178, #179)
- **`@drt_assets` decorator** — wraps `@multi_asset(can_subset=True)`, replacing the old `drt_assets()` function
- **`build_drt_asset_specs()`** — pure spec generation, decoupled from execution. Enables Dagster Pipes remote execution (#175)
- **`DagsterDrtResource`** — `ConfigurableResource` with `.run()` yielding `MaterializeResult` per sync. Auto-resolves `project_dir` from `@drt_assets` metadata
- **`DagsterDrtTranslator.get_asset_spec()`** — new primary override point (per-attribute methods deprecated)
- **`partitions_def`, `backfill_policy`, `pool`** support on `@drt_assets`
- **Asset `kinds`** metadata — `{"drt", "<destination_type>"}` visible in Dagster UI
- **Group name conflict detection** — raises if translator and decorator both set group names
- **Subset execution** — `DagsterDrtResource.run()` respects `context.selected_asset_keys`
- Old `drt_assets()` function renamed to `drt_assets_legacy()` with deprecation warning
- Requires `drt-core>=0.4.1`, `dagster>=1.6`

### [0.1.0] - 2026-04-01 (dagster-drt)

- First PyPI release (`pip install dagster-drt`)
- **DagsterDrtTranslator** (#127): Customise asset keys, group names, deps, and metadata (follows dagster-dbt pattern)
- **DrtConfig with dry_run** (#126): RunConfig controllable from Dagster UI, plus `drt_assets(dry_run=True)` for build-time defaults
- **MaterializeResult** (#128): Assets return structured metadata (rows_synced, rows_failed, rows_skipped, dry_run, row_errors_count)
- Requires `drt-core>=0.4.1`

## drt-core

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **Klaviyo event tracking** ([#1052](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1052)): the existing Klaviyo destination now supports `endpoint: event` alongside the backward-compatible `endpoint: profile` default, sending one warehouse-computed behavioral event per row to `POST /api/events/` in Klaviyo's JSON:API shape. Event names can come from `metric_name_field` or a constant `metric_name`; the existing email identifier and custom `properties_template` are reused. `unique_id_field` is required and must be non-blank in event mode, and every row must supply a non-blank value, so Klaviyo's timestamp-to-the-second default cannot silently collapse distinct events or make an ambiguous retry create a duplicate. Warehouse-native event controls are JSON-safe: datetime `time` values become ISO-8601 strings, date-only values are rejected with a row error because Klaviyo requires a time component, numeric `value` values become floats, and `unique_id` values become strings; the default properties fallback excludes configured metric/time/value/unique-ID control fields while profile-mode behavior remains unchanged. The default API revision is now `2026-01-15`; this behavior-relevant default change also affects the pre-existing profile endpoint, so existing configs that do not explicitly set `revision` will send a different `revision` header value. Calls retain the existing private-key/revision headers, shared retry and account rate-limiter paths, and treat the endpoint's documented `202 Accepted` as a normal success; docs distinguish its 350 req/s burst / 3500 per minute steady limits from profiles' 75 req/s / 700 per minute. Profile upsert and optional list membership remain unchanged; `sync.mode: mirror` remains out of scope.
- **Meta (Facebook) Conversions API destination** ([#1054](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1054)): new core `type: meta_conversions` connector posts warehouse events to `/{api_version}/{pixel_id}/events` in batches of at most 1000, authenticating with a long-lived access token in the `Authorization: Bearer` header rather than placing the credential in the URL/query parameters or using an OAuth2 client-credentials flow. Event names can be fixed or row-mapped; required row-mapped Unix-second event time, required Pixel+CAPI `event_id` deduplication, source URL, conversion value/currency, and configurable `action_source` are supported. `event_time_field` has no unmapped fallback: an early version defaulted to the current sync time when it was unset, which would have silently corrupted attribution/optimization data on any backfill, delayed batch, or replay by presenting historical rows as happening right now — caught before release ([#1077](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1077)) and closed by requiring an explicit, non-blank mapping (whitespace-stripped) instead, expressed as a genuinely required field in the config model rather than a validator-only check — so the generated JSON Schema and any MCP/YAML-editor tooling built on it also see it as required rather than optional-and-nullable. String/plain-number event IDs are emitted as strings, while containers, booleans, and non-finite floats (NaN/infinity) are rejected per row — a common missing-value sentinel that would otherwise collapse distinct rows onto the same deduplication key. Email is trim/lowercase-normalized and phone is reduced to ASCII digits before SHA-256; both hashes use Meta's one-item-list shape, while client IP, user agent, `fbc`, and `fbp` deliberately remain plain text. Identifier hashing is type-safe: email accepts only strings, phone accepts strings or losslessly stringified integers, and floats, booleans, and containers are rejected per row instead of being hashed into plausible-looking but meaningless values. Config requires at least one customer-information mapping, and each row is rejected locally unless its `event_id` and at least one mapped `user_data` identifier resolve to non-empty values; the default `action_source: website` additionally requires configured, non-empty `event_source_url` and `client_user_agent` values per row, while other action sources remain unrestricted. Event times older than Meta's seven-day acceptance window, non-finite conversion values, and any other values that cannot be encoded as strict JSON are rejected per row before sending, so one invalid value cannot poison its batch. Requests honor destination/sync retry and rate-limit precedence, acquire a limiter slot on every attempt, share the bucket per Pixel, and add a connector-scoped retry only when a 400 response explicitly contains `error.is_transient: true`; other 400 shapes still fail fast. The connector requires `event_id_field` so ambiguous retries can be deduplicated and fails a batch unless Meta returns an integer (non-boolean) `events_received` exactly matching the submitted count. The conservative configurable Graph API default is `v25.0`; versions expire and require periodic bumps. This first cut covers the core synchronous Pixel events endpoint — not Payload Helper or Events Manager UI workflows, and not app-event-specific fields beyond the generic `action_source` surface.
- **Stripe timestamped-signature verification for `drt serve`** ([#969](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/969)): `--auth hmac` gains `--hmac-scheme generic|stripe` (default `generic`, which is #854's behaviour unchanged) and `--hmac-tolerance` (default 300s). Stripe does not sign the body alone — its `Stripe-Signature` header is `t=<ts>,v1=<hex>[,v0=...]` and the signed payload is `<ts>.<body>` — so this is a separate verifier rather than a fourth encoding in the existing one, and the timestamp being inside the signature is what makes a replay window enforceable. Three details the scheme lives or dies on: **`v0` is ignored**, because Stripe attaches a deliberately fake one to test events and accepting any `v*` that matches would be a downgrade attack; **multiple `v1` signatures are accepted**, because rolling an endpoint secret leaves the old one live for up to 24h with one signature per active secret, and rejecting on first mismatch would drop deliveries through every rotation; and **the replay window is symmetric**, unlike Stripe's own libraries, which reject only stale timestamps and so leave a future-dated signature valid indefinitely. `--hmac-tolerance 0` is rejected — Stripe's docs call it out as disabling the recency check rather than tightening it. `--hmac-header` now defaults per scheme (`Stripe-Signature` under `stripe`), and an explicit value still wins. **POST only**: Stripe never sends a GET and so defines no signature shape for one, and `GET /runs/<id>` answers 401 under this scheme even if a `Stripe-Signature` header is supplied. That rejection is deliberate rather than incidental: a Stripe signature covers `<t>.<body>`, which on a bodyless GET carries no path, so accepting one would make a single captured header able to read any run id for the whole tolerance window — the replay [#998](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/998) had just closed for the generic scheme by binding GET signatures to the request path. Poll from the process that triggered the sync, or run a second listener with `--auth bearer`. Docs: `docs/guides/using-webhook-trigger.md`, including the raw-body/TLS-proxy caveat that causes most Stripe verification failures. Pub/Sub's OIDC JWT remains a separate follow-up ([#903](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/903)).

### Changed

- **`docs/guides/comparison.md` rewritten to match ADR 0011's positioning decision** ([#1053](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1053)): replaced the line-by-line feature-matrix table against Census/Hightouch/Polytomic (including a losing "22 vs 200+ destinations" row) with ADR 0011's single structural claim — drt never routes data through a drt-hosted intermediary — plus a "why not a UI/hosted runtime/audience builder" FAQ section closing the ADR's own follow-up item. Connector counts in the remaining factual "coverage" section corrected to the current 13 sources / 35 destinations (previously stale at 10/22-23; Meta Conversions in #1054 supplies the 35th). The tagline/README half of the ADR's follow-up was already shipped in #1010; this closes the remainder.

- **Internal — Snowflake/Databricks join `BaseSqlDestination`'s `finalize_sync()` template, closing the last piece of #720** ([#1030](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1030)): `finalize_sync()`'s swap-completion half (guard, shadow-name computation, connection/cursor lifecycle, dispatch) is now shared across Postgres/MySQL/Snowflake/Databricks instead of Snowflake/Databricks each carrying their own full copy. New per-dialect hooks (`_complete_swap`, `_reset_swap_state_after_completion`, `_reset_swap_state_after_noop`) preserve each dialect's exact recovery timing: Snowflake still leaves `_swap_shadow_created`/`_swap_table` intact after a failed `SWAP` so the shadow stays recoverable via `drt clean --orphans`, while Postgres/MySQL/Databricks still reset unconditionally — there's no equivalent recovery path for `INSERT OVERWRITE` to preserve. `_swap_direct_write` (the first-run-wrote-directly-to-target flag, which only exists on Snowflake/Databricks) is reset correctly in both the early no-op guard and the completion path via the same hooks, without being added to Postgres/MySQL's state. Mirror-mode dispatch is untouched — Databricks keeps its own `_finalize_mirror` override per #720's Phase 1 decision. No behavior change to any sync path.

- **The frozen source Protocols now describe third-party profiles honestly** ([#1034](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1034)): `Source.extract()` / `Source.test_connection()` and `IncrementalSource.extract_incremental()` previously accepted the closed 13-member `ProfileConfig` union even though [#997](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/997) / [#1011](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/1011) had already made plugin-registered source profiles loadable at runtime by force-casting an arbitrary plugin instance into that union. That made the stable Protocol declaration inaccurate at the exact extension boundary ADR 0007 freezes. The methods now accept a runtime-checkable structural `ProfileConfigLike` containing only the two operations drt-core actually uses generically (`type` and `describe()`); all 13 built-in profile dataclasses satisfy it unchanged, and their source implementations keep the established first-line `assert isinstance(config, ConcreteProfile)` narrowing pattern. `load_profile()` now returns that honest structural type and validates the plugin instance instead of casting it, while the generic dispatch/execution path carries the same type through to extraction. A new plugin-authoring guide documents the required method signature and why annotating a plugin method directly with its concrete profile class violates Protocol parameter contravariance. This closes the source-side typing gap left after the plugin config work contributed by [@Muawiya-contact](https://github.com/Muawiya-contact).

- **Internal — RowError recording centralized across 23 destinations** ([#722](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/722)): the `try/except → result.failed += 1 → row_errors.append(RowError(...))` idiom, duplicated roughly 100 times across the destination layer, now goes through a shared `record_row_error()` helper (`drt/destinations/row_errors.py`). Each site keeps computing its own `record_preview` (a JSON dict dump for most destinations, `str()` of an already-transformed positional bind list for a few warehouse ones) and its own `on_error` control flow (`break`/`continue`/`return`/`raise`, whichever it already used) — only the 3-line "record the failure" block moved. `postgres.py`/`mysql.py` keep their own working `BaseSqlDestination._record_row_error`, unchanged; `clickhouse.py` was deliberately left alone (an unrelated in-flight PR was actively changing its error handling) for a follow-up sweep. No behavior change.

- **Internal — Snowflake/Databricks join `BaseSqlDestination`, phase 1** ([#720](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/720)): both classes now subclass the same dialect-agnostic base Postgres/MySQL already use (#719), implementing only the `_dialect_connect`/`_qualify_ident` hooks this phase needs — `load()` and `finalize_sync()` stay their own concrete overrides, unchanged. `_dialect_connect`'s signature widened to accept query tags (Postgres/MySQL accept and ignore the new parameter). Snowflake's and Databricks' own row-error-recording, previously handled inline, now go through the same shared `record_row_error()` helper as the entry above (#722), preserving each dialect's existing `record_preview` basis exactly. Fixed a real, previously-silent gap found while scoping this: `drt state reset --tracked-mirror` against a Snowflake or Databricks destination would have started raising an unhandled `NotImplementedError` once `reset_tracked_state` became inherited-but-unimplemented, instead of the existing clean "does not support tracked mirror" message — the CLI and MCP `state_reset` tool now detect this case explicitly and keep degrading gracefully. No behavior change to any sync path. Design decisions (why Databricks doesn't adopt `_build_mirror_delete`, why `finalize_sync`'s swap-commit ordering stays dialect-specific) are recorded on the issue.

- **ClickHouse destination batches inserts instead of one `client.insert()` call per record** ([#961](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/961)): ClickHouse is a columnar warehouse built for bulk inserts — one-row-at-a-time inserts are the documented anti-pattern its own docs warn against (many small inserts create many parts, triggering merge pressure). Batches by `sync.batch_size` (default 100, no new config, now validated as `> 0` — a `0` or negative value previously either crashed ungracefully or silently inserted nothing while reporting success). `client.insert()`'s HTTP transport isn't a SQL transaction, so a failed batch is ambiguous — the request may have never reached ClickHouse, or it may have committed and only the response was lost; retrying naively would duplicate every row in the latter case. A failed batch is retried once with the same `insert_deduplication_token` (a safe no-op if it already committed) before falling back to today's row-by-row `RowError` attribution, so `on_error: skip`/`fail` semantics and mirror-mode key/scope accumulation are unchanged. **This retry is best-effort, not a guarantee**: `insert_deduplication_token` only takes effect if the table's dedup window is enabled server-side — on by default for `Replicated*MergeTree`, off by default for a plain non-replicated table — so operators who need a hard guarantee against duplicate rows from an ambiguous HTTP failure should enable `non_replicated_deduplication_window` on their table; drt does not verify or configure it. `mode: replace` + `replace_strategy: swap` gets the same batching. 250 rows at the default batch size: 250 → 3 `client.insert()` calls.

- **Internal — Snowflake/Databricks join `BaseSqlDestination`, phase 2** ([#720](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/720)): `load()` is now the shared dialect-agnostic template both classes inherit from the base (the same one Postgres/MySQL already use), replacing each dialect's own full `load()` override. `_load_replace`, `_load_replace_swap`, and `_load_upsert` are now real per-dialect hooks matching the base's contract — the former inline `config.mode` (`insert`/`merge`, with mirror forcing `merge`) dispatch moved inside each `_load_upsert`, and the mirror-key/scope accumulation duplicated in both files' old `load()` bodies is gone in favor of the base's existing `_accumulate_mirror_state`. Each dialect overrides `_validate_mirror_scope` to keep calling its existing `check_mirror_supported(...)` check (stricter than the base's default — it also validates `upsert_key` truthiness and dialect scope-tracking support), so mirror-mode validation behavior is unchanged. Pure code motion: no SQL statement, parameter binding, chunk-sizing (Snowflake's MERGE chunk budget, Databricks' native 255-marker INSERT chunking), or `on_error`/`RowError` behavior changed — every dialect-specific test asserting exact statement/param shape still passes unmodified (only mock-cursor fixture setup changed, to match the base's cursor lifecycle). `finalize_sync()`, `_finalize_mirror()`, `_finalize_mirror_tracked()`, and the mirror-delete machinery are untouched — that's phase 3 (Snowflake only) and permanently out of scope for Databricks, per the design decisions recorded on the issue.

- **Internal — Snowflake joins the shared mirror-finalize template, phase 3** ([#720](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/720)): Snowflake now inherits `BaseSqlDestination._finalize_mirror()` and `_finalize_mirror_tracked()` instead of carrying wholesale copies. Its existing mirror DELETE builder is the shared `_build_mirror_delete` hook, and six Snowflake state-table hooks preserve the existing fully-qualified `SHOW TABLES` / `information_schema` / DDL statements. Narrow base hooks retain Snowflake's autocommit/no-savepoint behavior, fully-qualified scratch table, `VARCHAR` scratch schema, and list bindings while leaving the Postgres/MySQL wire SQL unchanged. Those state hooks also make the base's existing `drt state reset --tracked-mirror` implementation available to Snowflake. `finalize_sync()` remains Snowflake-specific for atomic `SWAP WITH`; Databricks remains permanently outside this phase because its staged-key mirror deletion does not fit the base's single-statement builder contract. No mirror-run wire-SQL or sync behavior change.

### Fixed

- **Snowflake source profiles configured for key-pair auth via `~/.drt/profiles.yml` silently connected with no credential at all** ([#1092](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1092)): `load_profile()`'s hand-written `snowflake` branch never read `private_key_env` / `private_key_passphrase_env` from the raw YAML mapping, even though `docs/connectors/snowflake.md` documents both as applying to the source profile and #737 shipped key-pair auth specifically because new Snowflake accounts enforce MFA on password sign-ins. `drt profile add`'s raw-dict write path (`write_raw_profile()`) persisted the fields to disk correctly; only the typed load path used at actual sync time silently dropped them, so a profile set up exactly as documented reproducibly connected with `private_key_pem=None` instead of the configured key pair. `save_profile()` had the matching gap on write — a profile round-tripped through the typed path (`save_profile()` then `load_profile()`) also lost both fields. Both now read/write alongside the existing `password_env` handling. Found while auditing `load_profile()`/`save_profile()` for [#402](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/402)'s registry-based refactor; live-reproduced before fixing (both new regression tests fail on the prior code). A second gap found in the same audit — six profile types' `fetch_size` field is likewise never read from or written to `profiles.yml` despite being documented as configurable — is a separate, lower-severity issue tracked as [#1093](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1093) rather than fixed here.

- **`fetch_size` in `profiles.yml` was documented but never read or written** ([#1093](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1093)): the six streaming SQL profile types (DuckDB, SQLite, Postgres, Redshift, Snowflake, SQL Server) all define a `fetch_size` field — and the source connectors honor it via `arraysize`/`itersize`/`fetchmany` — but `load_profile()` ignored the key entirely and `save_profile()` never emitted it, so a user who set `fetch_size: 2500` in `profiles.yml` got silently ignored and could never discover the drift. `load_profile()` now reads `fetch_size` for all six types (falling back to `DEFAULT_FETCH_SIZE`, matching the dataclass default), and `save_profile()` writes it back only when it differs from the default, preserving the existing terse-file convention for the other fields. No behavior change for existing profiles: an absent key still resolves to 10000, and a default-valued profile still round-trips without an explicit `fetch_size` line.

- **An incremental sync with `on_error: fail` could advance its watermark past rows that were never actually loaded** ([#1074](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1074), [#1084](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1084)): the engine tracked a batch's max cursor value *before* attempting to load/enrich it, so a fatal failure partway through a batch — a `destination.load()` failure, a `finalize()` failure on a staged destination, or a fatal (`on_miss: fail`) lookup miss under `apply_lookups()` — still persisted a cursor that covered rows never actually delivered. A retry would then skip them forever, since the incremental extraction predicate reads from the already-advanced watermark.

  On any of those three fatal conditions, the engine now reverts the cursor to wherever the run started rather than persisting anything tracked during the failed run — the only rollback that's safe regardless of extraction order, since drt does not guarantee `cursor_field`-ordered extraction and a narrower, per-batch rollback can't rule out an unconsumed later batch (or, for lookups, an unconsumed later row in the same batch) holding a lower or tied cursor value. Accepted trade-off: retrying re-sends every row from batches that already succeeded earlier in the same failed run, which for a destination without natural replay safety (Slack, email) means a duplicate message rather than just a duplicate warehouse upsert — judged the lesser failure mode next to silently and permanently losing rows. The rollback target is resolved independently of any `--cursor-value` override: the override is a documented one-run backfill/recovery value, so a failed override run reverts to the durable pre-run watermark, not to the override itself, and that resolution is skipped entirely for `--dry-run` (which never persists anything) so a preview doesn't newly depend on a remote watermark backend being reachable. `on_error: skip` is unaffected by construction. The fatal-lookup case has one known gap: unlike a `destination.load()` failure, the missed row's `RowError` doesn't reach the DLQ (`drt retry`) — it only comes back via re-extraction once the watermark reverts.

  `StatePersistingObserver` also now refuses to ever move a persisted cursor backward or wipe it to `None`, regardless of source — a rollback handing it an older value than what's already stored, or an ordinary race where a second run of the same sync commits a newer watermark while this one is still failing. This is a read-then-write guard, not a compare-and-swap, so a narrow cross-process race window remains; closing it needs the generation-token primitive [#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756) is already building for remote state backends — noted on that issue rather than duplicated here.

- **Generated sync/project schemas are deterministic across the supported pydantic range** ([#1081](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1081)): `generate_sync_schema()` now reapplies the live registered-plugin destination enum after pydantic generation, fixing a pydantic 2.5--2.10 hook-composition bug that silently removed plugin-type typo detection across roughly half of drt-core's supported `pydantic>=2.5,<3` range. A custom documented schema generator also normalizes single-value `Literal` fields and permissive `dict[str, Any]` fields to pydantic 2.13.5's canonical shape, closing the remaining byte-level drift without pinning drt-core's runtime dependency or freezing live plugin reflection. `tests/unit/test_schema_pydantic_compat.py` now locks those exact shapes and the top-level plugin-reflection path for future schema-generator bumps.

- **`staged_upload`'s rate limiter was keyed off the wrong host — and, before the final fix, off no host at all for a templated poll URL** ([#1068](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1068)): #1048 wired the poll phase into the same rate limiter as stage/trigger, but the limiter identity was still always derived from `trigger.url`'s host. The common case (poll shares the trigger's host) worked, but `poll.url` is independently configurable — a destination polling a genuinely different host either got jointly rate-limited with unrelated syncs on that trigger host, or shared a limiter with an unrelated destination that happened to trigger against the same host its own poll used. `resolve_rate_limiter()` gains an optional `key_override` parameter (every other destination's call site is unaffected — the parameter defaults to the existing `config.rate_limit_key()` behavior), and `staged_upload.finalize()` now renders `poll.url` against the trigger-response context *before* resolving the limiter and keys it from the actual rendered host — correctly unifying two configs whose static trigger hosts differ but whose runtime-resolved poll host is the same vendor endpoint, and correctly separating two configs whose rendered poll hosts differ even if their trigger hosts happen to match. Two rounds of Codex adversarial review before merge: the first shipped a static, config-only fix (prefer `poll.url`'s unrendered host, fall back to `trigger.url`) that broke for a `poll.url` supplied *entirely* by the trigger response (e.g. `poll.url: "{{ status_url }}"`, a vendor-returned status URL) — the unrendered string has no parseable host, so every such destination collapsed onto one shared, incorrectly-keyed limiter bucket; the second confirmed the render-before-keying fix resolves it correctly, including cross-checking that two different rendered poll hosts don't collide even when their trigger hosts do. Host-derived keying remains an approximation of vendor quota identity — over-sharing (a multi-tenant vendor centralizing its polling host) is the documented safe default per `DescribableConfig.rate_limit_key()`'s policy (over-sharing only paces more conservatively; it cannot cause a 429), but under-sharing (one account served by genuinely per-job hostnames, not just per-job paths) remains possible and unaddressed here — tracked as a separate opt-in override field in [#1089](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1089) rather than expanding this fix's scope.

- **`drt run --dry-run --diff`'s Snowflake leg could misreport unchanged rows as updated for any non-key column, not just the ones #1062/#1063 had already covered** ([#1064](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1064)): `fetch_rows()`'s case-folding reconciliation (Snowflake uppercase-folds unquoted identifiers) only hinted `upsert_key`'s casing, so `compute_diff()`'s `changed_fields()` comparison — which runs over every column, not just the key — saw the destination's folded, lowercased non-key column and the source's differently-cased field as two unrelated columns and reported a spurious difference on every row that had one, live-confirmed by reverting the fix and rerunning the new regression test (2/2 identical rows misreported as updated; 1/1 genuinely-changed row correctly reported after the fix). The hint (renamed `key_hint` → `field_hint` throughout `drt/destinations/query.py`) now covers every field in the source record, not just the key — built as the union across *every* record plus `upsert_key`, not read from the first record alone: caught in Codex adversarial review, since `dry_run_records` accumulates across the whole run and a field or an uppercase-configured key can legitimately first appear in a later record, which an earlier version of this fix (reading only `records[0]`) would have missed, silently reproducing the same collapse it was meant to close. This union is used only by the full-table-scan fetch path (replace mode, or a keyed fetch's `NotImplemented` fallback); the ordinary keyed-fetch column list deliberately keeps reading `records[0]` alone, matching `BaseSqlDestination.load()`'s own per-batch column selection — a further Codex review round found that widening the keyed fetch to the union would have made the dry-run preview fetch (and could report a phantom diff on) fields the real write silently drops on a heterogeneous batch, so the two paths intentionally diverge on this point rather than sharing one field set. Separately, MySQL's own leg needed no equivalent fix: a live `local_sql_smoke` test against a real MySQL 8.4 container confirms mixed-case column names round-trip exactly (MySQL's `lower_case_table_names` affects table/database name storage, not column names), closing that half of the issue as verified-non-reproducing rather than assumed-fine from documentation alone. Databricks' half of the issue needs the equivalent live `dwh_smoke` verification, which requires real credentials not available in this pass — left open.

- **Klaviyo profile/event `properties` crashed on common warehouse driver types** ([#1076](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1076)): `_properties()`'s default direct-copy path (any unmapped field, used whenever `properties_template` isn't configured) passed `Decimal`, `date`, `datetime`, `time`, `timedelta`, and non-finite `float` (`NaN`/`inf`) values straight through — none of these are JSON-serializable, or (for `NaN`/`inf`) only serializable via a non-standard extension `httpx`'s encoder explicitly disables (`allow_nan=False`) — so a single such value raised before any request was sent, and under the default `on_error: fail` stopped the rest of the batch. Values are now recursively normalized (`date`/`datetime`/`time` → `.isoformat()`, `timedelta` — e.g. PyMySQL's representation of a MySQL `TIME` column, including negative/>24h durations — → `.total_seconds()`, non-finite `float` → a clear per-row error) including inside nested dict/list values (e.g. JSON/STRUCT-typed source columns), for both the profile and event endpoints. `Decimal` conversion is precision-safe rather than a blind `float()`, and always emits the same JSON number type for every value from a given column: a `Decimal` that can't round-trip exactly through `float` (too large/precise, or non-finite) raises a per-row error instead of silently rounding, overflowing to `inf`, or falling back to a string. As a backstop against the next not-yet-discovered driver type, `_properties()` now also validates its own output against `json.dumps(..., allow_nan=False)` — the exact encoder `httpx` uses — before returning, so any remaining unhandled type surfaces as one clear per-row error instead of a cryptic failure deep inside request encoding. Four rounds of Codex adversarial review before merge, each catching a real, further gap: a fallback-to-string path that would have made one warehouse `DECIMAL` column emit a mix of JSON number and string values depending on the individual row (silently changing how Klaviyo's segment builder evaluates that property); `time` and non-finite native `float`; and `timedelta`.

- **Internal — Ruff formatting drift is now prevented** ([#944](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/944)): Ruff is pinned to 0.16.5 in both the dev extra and pre-commit config, the existing `drt/` and `tests/` drift has been reformatted once, and CI now runs `ruff format --check drt tests` after linting. The formatting-only commit is recorded in `.git-blame-ignore-revs`; contributors who want blame to skip it can run `git config blame.ignoreRevsFile .git-blame-ignore-revs` locally.
- **VS Code schema regeneration is reproducible across local and CI environments** ([#1070](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1070)): pydantic is pinned exactly because its version affects the JSON Schema emitted for the callable `Discriminator` used by the destination union — but only in a new, dedicated `schema-gen` extra, not the published runtime dependency range or the shared `dev` extra (pinning either of those would either risk unsatisfiable dependency resolution for embedding apps, or silently stop the main test matrix from exercising `pydantic>=2.5,<3`). Only the schema-drift CI job and `regenerate-schemas.sh` install `[schema-gen]`. The workflow also prints the resolved pydantic version before regenerating the bundled schemas, and now re-runs on `pyproject.toml` changes so a routine dependency bump can't merge without re-checking the bundled schemas.

- **Object-store history retention tests no longer expire with the wall clock** ([#1066](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1066)): prune/cap fixtures now derive ordered run timestamps from the test-run time instead of hardcoding August 2026 dates, preventing clean `main` CI from changing its expected removed count as the calendar advances.

- **Staged Upload and Salesforce Bulk now honor `sync.retry` and `sync.rate_limit` while polling job status** ([#1048](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1048)): both destination configs now also accept destination-level `retry` / `rate_limit` overrides, and their safe, idempotent status checks use the shared retry and process-wide rate-limiter helpers. Every physical retry attempt now acquires its own process-wide rate-limit slot. Staged Upload retries only `GET` / `HEAD` polls; free-form mutating poll methods remain one-shot to avoid replaying side effects. Poll timeouts bound retry backoff too: each invocation clamps `max_backoff` and each individual request's own network timeout to the remaining budget, checks the deadline before retrying, and rejects even a successful response that arrives late — tightening the worst case substantially, though not a mathematically exact deadline across every retry attempt (bounding that fully would require changes to the shared retry helper used by ~20 other destinations, tracked separately if it's ever needed). Initial stage/trigger/job-create/upload/close calls remain unretried because replaying those mutating requests after an ambiguous failure could create duplicate jobs or uploads; Salesforce's failed-results download is unchanged because it is not part of the status-polling leg scoped by this fix.

- **BigQuery, Airtable, and Klaviyo now pass `drt validate --check-connection` with their documented least-privilege credentials** ([#1059](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1059)): all three connectors were excluded from #1049's structural `ConnectionTestable` dispatch because their probes needed broader API scope than each connector's own docs recommend as the minimal write-only credential. Fixed with a real least-privilege probe per API, verified against each vendor's current documentation rather than guessed: BigQuery's `test_connection()` now calls `get_table()` (needs only `bigquery.tables.get`, already included in the `roles/bigquery.dataEditor` role that grants the documented `bigquery.tables.updateData`) instead of running a `SELECT 1` query job (needs the separate, broader `bigquery.jobs.create`). Airtable's now calls `GET /v0/meta/whoami`, Airtable's own documented scope-free identity endpoint, instead of reading a record (`data.records:read`). Klaviyo has no scope-free identity endpoint, so it still probes `GET /accounts/` but now also accepts Klaviyo's documented `403` + `code: "permission_denied"` response as proof of a valid (if more narrowly scoped) key, rather than treating every non-2xx as a failure — any other error still raises normally. All three are back in the normal `ConnectionTestable` dispatch; no exclusion list needed.

- **Two more `drt run --dry-run --diff` preview correctness bugs, both pre-existing before #1044** ([#1061](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1061), [#1062](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1062)): (1) the tracked-mirror preview ignored `sync.mirror.scope` entirely — a run touching one tenant/parent could preview another tenant's stale keys as deletions, contradicting what the real `_finalize_mirror_tracked()` actually does. Now filters prior state to the scopes this run's source records actually produced, matching the real finalizer, across all five SQL dialects; unscoped tracked mirror is unaffected. (2) `compute_diff()`'s replace-mode full-table scan calls `fetch_rows(..., columns=[])`, and Postgres/MySQL/Snowflake/ClickHouse's readers built result dicts via `dict(zip([], row))` — always `{}` — silently collapsing every destination row into one keyless entry and severely understating a real `replace` run's deletions (Databricks was already fixed for this in #1044). All four now derive column names from query metadata (`cursor.description` / ClickHouse's `column_names`) when `columns` is empty, matching Databricks' existing fix.

- **`drt run --dry-run --diff` now previews every mirror DELETE strategy on Snowflake, ClickHouse, and Databricks** ([#1044](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1044)): the read-only tracked-state and destination-key helpers previously covered only Postgres/MySQL, despite all five SQL destinations supporting both mirror strategies. Each warehouse now reads through its existing connection, state-table location, identifier, and parameter conventions; Databricks also gains the minimal read-only query capability needed to reach true-diff mode instead of sample mode. A failed mirror-only read no longer renders identically to a successful zero-delete preview: text reports `preview unavailable`, while JSON exposes `delete_preview_unavailable_reason` (`null` after a successful read, including zero deletes). No mirror finalization or DELETE statement changed. Two rounds of review caught real defects in the new Databricks/ClickHouse code: Databricks queries built without chunking could exceed its 255-native-parameter-marker limit in both the mirror-scope read and the primary keyed diff lookup (now capped via the same `_rows_per_chunk` math every other Databricks write path uses); ClickHouse's key SELECT returned raw-typed values that could compare unequal to the source's plain value for typed columns like UUID (now wrapped in `toString()`, matching the real DELETE's own comparison — scoped to ClickHouse only, since coercing every dialect this way turned out to be its own bug, breaking numeric-type comparisons elsewhere); and enabling Databricks' new query capability surfaced a pre-existing `columns=[]`-collapses-every-row-to-`{}` gap in the replace-mode full-scan path (fixed for Databricks by deriving columns from cursor metadata; the same gap on Postgres/MySQL/Snowflake/ClickHouse is pre-existing and tracked separately, [#1062](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1062)). A third finding — the tracked-mirror preview ignoring `mirror.scope` — is also pre-existing (confirmed live on Postgres/MySQL since #693) and unrelated to this PR's scope; tracked as [#1061](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1061).

- **`drt validate --check-connection` now tests every `ConnectionTestable` destination, not just 4 hardcoded SQL types** ([#1049](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1049)): `_run_connection_test()` gated on an `isinstance(dest_config, (Postgres, MySQL, ClickHouse, Snowflake))` tuple before ever checking the `ConnectionTestable` Protocol — Databricks, which already implements `test_connection()`, was reported as skipped instead of actually tested. Now dispatches solely on `isinstance(dest, ConnectionTestable)`. A destination genuinely lacking the capability is now reported as skipped rather than (for the 4 previously-hardcoded types) a hard failure — "doesn't implement this optional capability" was never really a *failure*. Caught in review: BigQuery, Airtable, and Klaviyo also already implemented `test_connection()`, but each probes broader API scope than what its own docs recommend as the minimal write-only credential (BigQuery's `SELECT 1` needs `bigquery.jobs.create`, which append-mode's documented `bigquery.tables.updateData` doesn't grant; Klaviyo/Airtable need read scopes their write-only docs don't ask for) — wiring them into this structural dispatch would have reported a false failure for a correctly, minimally scoped setup. All three keep their existing `test_connection()` unchanged (renaming an already-shipped public method imported from `drt.*` is itself a breaking change per `VERSIONING.md`) — instead, `_run_connection_test()` excludes these three specific classes explicitly before the structural check, so `drt validate --check-connection` behavior for them is unchanged from before this PR. Tracked as [#1059](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1059).

- **`drt docs serve` gives a clean CLI error instead of a raw `NotImplementedError`; `docs/llm/CONTEXT.md`'s mirror-support claim updated** ([#1043](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1043)): the stub's message claimed "scheduled for v0.8.x" — stale, and never built — now points at `drt docs generate --format html`/`--inline` as the actual way to get a browsable catalog. Separately, the LLM-facing context doc still said tracked+scoped mirror was Postgres/MySQL-only and not combinable, stale since v0.8.4 added combined support on Snowflake/ClickHouse/Databricks too.

- **`sync.mode: replace` / `mirror` now fail fast on destinations that don't implement them, instead of silently running a normal load** ([#1042](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1042)): no capability check existed anywhere in the write path — a Slack, HubSpot, BigQuery, or any other non-SQL/non-`ClickHouse` destination configured with `sync.mode: mirror` reported success while never deleting stale destination data; `mode: replace` silently behaved as a normal upsert/append instead. New optional `ModeCapable` Protocol (`supported_modes() -> frozenset[str]`, mirroring the existing `MatchPolicyCapable` pattern from #757) lets Postgres/MySQL/Snowflake/Databricks/ClickHouse — the only destinations with real replace/mirror machinery today — declare it, each individually rather than via a shared base class default (`BaseSqlDestination`'s replace/mirror hooks are abstract, so an incomplete future subclass must not silently inherit a capability it can't serve — caught in review). `_check_mode_supported()` in the engine now rejects an undeclared `replace`/`mirror` before any extraction or write happens, mirroring `_check_match_policy_supported`'s existing fail-fast placement and framing (its own docstring already assumed this check existed for mirror — it didn't, until now). `full` / `incremental` / `upsert` need no capability declaration. S3/GCS/Azure Blob also declare it — their connector docs already document `sync.mode` as a no-op for these one-object-per-run destinations, so declaring it (rather than leaving it undeclared) preserves that existing, documented contract instead of turning it into a new breaking failure on upgrade (also caught in review). `sync.unit_tests`' `CaptureDestination` stand-in (#780) now accepts every mode unconditionally too — it substitutes for the real destination when replaying a fixture, so gating it the same way would have failed every `replace`/`mirror` unit test as a false negative even when the real configured destination fully supports the mode (also caught in review — a unit test verifies the transform pipeline, not destination write mechanics). No behavior change for any currently-shipped destination.

- **`drt retry` handles staged destinations instead of calling a missing `load()` method** ([#1037](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1037)): replaying an existing DLQ entry for a sync whose destination is (or has since become) `salesforce_bulk`, `staged_upload`, or another `StagedDestination` previously crashed with `AttributeError`. Retry now stages each configured chunk and finalizes the accumulated batch once, removes only records that the final result attributes as successful, and conservatively keeps the full retry set queued when failures cannot be mapped to individual records. Per-record attribution via `RowError.batch_index` is trusted only when the whole retry set fit in a single `stage()` call — `StagedDestination`'s Protocol doesn't define whether that index is chunk-local or global across multiple `stage()` calls, so trusting it across chunks risked silently misattributing a later chunk's failure to an earlier chunk's record (caught in review); `salesforce_bulk` additionally never trusts it at all, since its failed-results CSV reports index 0 for every failure regardless of chunk count. A staged `finalize()` exception is returned as a clear failed retry with every attempted entry retained; this does not add new DLQ capture for failures from ordinary staged-destination sync runs.

- **Local watermark writes are safe across overlapping drt processes** ([#1033](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1033)): `LocalWatermarkStorage` was the local state store #963 missed — unlike `LocalStateManager`, `LocalHistoryManager`, and `LocalDlqStore`, it had neither their instance-local `threading.Lock` nor the blocking OS advisory sidecar lock #963 added. Concurrent syncs could therefore read the same `watermarks.json` snapshot and let a later save or delete silently discard the other process's watermark update. Local watermark reads now use the same per-instance thread lock as the other stores, while save/delete hold both locks across their full read-modify-write cycle. The blocking local lock waits out contention and therefore does not invent GCS's optimistic-retry `WatermarkContentionError` failure mode.

- **Local run state, history retention, and DLQ writes are safe across overlapping drt processes** ([#963](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/963)): `LocalStateManager`, `LocalHistoryManager`, and `LocalDlqStore` previously protected whole-file read-modify-write cycles with only an instance-local `threading.Lock`, so two CLI processes could both read the same snapshot and let the later write silently discard the other's update — including the `drt run` / `drt retry` race left open by #955. Mutating state saves/resets, history append/prune, and DLQ append/replace/reconcile now hold a blocking stdlib OS advisory lock on a persistent `<data-file>.lock` sidecar for the full read-modify-write span, while retaining the existing thread lock for same-instance workers. Sidecars make first-run files lockable before they exist; POSIX uses `flock` and Windows uses a one-byte `msvcrt.locking` range, with no new core dependency. History append's own write is still a single `O_APPEND` call (POSIX-atomic against other appends on its own), but it now takes the same sidecar lock retention pruning does — `O_APPEND` alone doesn't protect an append against a concurrent prune's read-then-`tmp.replace()` rewrite, which would otherwise silently drop an append landing in that window; caught while reviewing this fix, since `--threads` and cross-process racing make the append+immediate-prune sequence in `run_sync()` a realistic overlap, not just a theoretical one. Single-writer-at-a-time remains the recommended operational model, and network filesystems still depend on their own advisory-lock semantics.

## [0.10.0] - 2026-08-28

### Fixed

- **`drt serve --auth hmac`: a `GET` signature could read any run, and could be replayed to trigger any sync** ([#936](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/936)). `GET /runs/<id>` signed the empty body, so one signature was a constant per secret: a header leaked from a proxy log opened every run id, not the one it was issued for. The signed payload now includes the request path, binding a signature to the single run it was minted for. Two things fell out of review and are fixed here rather than shipped. **The path is keyed off the HTTP method, not off an empty body** — `POST /sync/<name>` is documented as the normal bodyless way to trigger a sync, and inferring from emptiness would have started `401`ing every existing client that correctly signs `HMAC(secret, b"")`; that contract is unchanged. **And the GET path is signed under a separate derived key**, `HMAC(secret, "drt/serve/v1/get-path")`, not the raw secret. Sharing one key would have been strictly worse than the bug being fixed: an attacker chooses a POST body, so they could send the GET's signed payload *as* a body and reuse its digest, turning a leaked read-only signature into the ability to trigger an arbitrary sync. No prefix or separator can close that, since a body is free bytes — only a distinct key can. Covered by tests asserting the exploit directly: a signature for run A rejected against run B, and a captured GET signature rejected against `POST /sync/<other>` for every body shape crafted from the signed path. Docs: `docs/guides/using-webhook-trigger.md` (both the POST and GET `openssl` examples now run as written).

- **`type: file` silently retained only the final batch of every multi-batch sync** ([#1002](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1002)): `FileDestination.load()` is called once per engine batch, but its CSV, JSON, and JSONL writers reopened the configured path in truncate mode every time. Any export whose row count exceeded `sync.batch_size` therefore reported the full `rows_extracted` / `SyncResult.success` count while silently losing every row except those in the final batch — often the majority of the data. A `FileDestination` now keeps per-instance, per-path write state for the lifetime of its sync: CSV writes its header once and appends later batches (failing clearly if a later record's columns differ), while JSONL appends its independently valid lines after the first batch. Single-array JSON cannot be appended without breaking its top-level `[...]` syntax, so that format deliberately buffers the full sync in memory and rewrites the complete valid array after each batch; this known memory cost is specific to `format: json`, and JSONL remains the streaming-friendly choice for large exports. The connector remains a plain `Destination`, preserving its existing per-batch `on_error: fail` early-stop behavior. A new `finalize_sync()` hook clears this write-state after each sync (caught in Codex review) — the CLI/engine path always constructs a fresh `FileDestination` per run so this never fires there, but a library caller reusing one instance across multiple `run_sync()` calls would otherwise have the second run silently append to or fold in the first run's records instead of truncating.

- **`sync.mode: mirror` on Snowflake required schema-level `CREATE TABLE` even for a least-privilege role, the gap v0.9.1's #986 fix explicitly left open** ([#988](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/988)). The write path unconditionally staged every mirror write through `CREATE TEMP TABLE` before `MERGE`ing it in — independent of, and unaffected by, #986/#987's state-diff fix, which only touched the read side. Replaced with `MERGE INTO ... USING (SELECT ... FROM (VALUES ...) AS t(...))`: the batch's rows source the `MERGE` directly, chunked to stay under a bind-parameter budget verified live against a real account (1000 rows / 3 columns / 3000 params completed in under a second with no degradation across 10/25/50/100/250/500/1000-row checkpoints; an earlier, cruder probe found a single unbounded statement in the thousands-of-rows range became impractically slow, confirming chunking is required, not optional). A chunk that fails outright falls back to one `MERGE` per row within it, preserving the same per-row `on_error` isolation the old per-row staging INSERT provided — a bulk chunk failure no longer takes every row in it down together. JSON/VARIANT columns get `PARSE_JSON` applied in the outer `SELECT` that projects the `VALUES` table rather than inside `VALUES()` itself, since Snowflake disallows functions there (the same constraint plain `INSERT` already works around for #317's Layer 3). `sync.mode: mirror` — tracked or not — is now genuinely no-DDL end to end on Snowflake for a role with only `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`MERGE`/`DELETE` on the target and pre-provisioned state tables. Docs: `docs/connectors/snowflake.md`.

### Added

- **Protocol freeze policy documented for v1.0** ([#304](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/304), [ADR 0007](docs/adr/0007-protocol-stability-policy.md)): all 21 Protocol class docstrings now state whether the interface is stable or internal, ADR 0007's freeze-scope table now includes the four extension points added after its original review, and `VERSIONING.md` links to the canonical policy while making the two-minor-version deprecation floor plus next-MAJOR removal rule explicit. This prepares the public Protocol freeze for the planned v1.0 release in November 2026; it does not activate the freeze early or claim that v1.0 has shipped. Documentation and annotations only — no runtime behavior changes.

- **Product boundary accepted: four things drt-core's OSS core will not build** ([ADR 0011](docs/adr/0011-subtraction-positioning-vs-reverse-etl.md)): corrects an earlier framing — dlt and dbt are adjacent pipeline stages, not competitors; drt's actual competitive set is commercial reverse-ETL tools (Census, Hightouch, RudderStack Reverse ETL, Polytomic, and similar), which near-universally bundle the sync engine with a web UI, a hosted/metered runtime, an audience/segmentation builder, and a proprietary connector catalog. drt-core deliberately excludes all four — a web UI was already an unstated rule ([#297](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/297)'s plugin discovery is the answer to a proprietary catalog, not a UI), audience-building stays a SQL/dbt-modeling problem, and no hosted runtime means no per-row billing is structural, not a pricing choice. Mirrors [ADR 0008](docs/adr/0008-enterprise-boundary-rbac-and-audit-hooks.md)'s shape exactly: a boundary on `drt-core` as OSS, not on drt as a company — a UI or managed runtime could still exist in a separate, closed product built on top of drt-core, the same relationship Enterprise RBAC has to `PermissionChecker`. Governance/positioning only: gives future feature-request triage a written boundary to cite (a UI or audience-builder PR should be declined by default, the same way an RBAC PR is declined by default citing ADR 0008). Positioning copy (README tagline, repo description) is intentionally left as a follow-up, not done in this ADR.

- **Cross-process rate-limit coordination is pluggable, while the default stays unchanged** ([#921](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/921), [ADR 0012](docs/adr/0012-cross-process-rate-limit-coordination.md)): #769's endpoint-keyed registry coordinates `--threads N` inside one process, but an orchestrator launching N separate `drt run` processes still creates N buckets and can put N× the configured rate on one endpoint. The existing local `RateLimiter`, pacing algorithm, `rate_limit` precedence, vendor ceilings, burst semantics, min-wins tightening, and keyed in-process cache are untouched. Its implicit `acquire()` / `tighten_to()` shape is now the public, runtime-checkable `RateLimiterBackend` Protocol, with a single-active-factory registry (`register_rate_limiter_backend` / `get_rate_limiter_backend`) and a new `drt.rate_limiter_backends` entry-point group. A third-party package can therefore supply coordination backed by infrastructure the operator already runs. `LimiterFactory` gains a required, keyword-only `key` parameter (`config.rate_limit_key()`) — the identity a distributed backend needs to derive its own shared bucket name — and every destination's `limiter_factory=RateLimiter` (originally passed only so `patch(".../RateLimiter")` keeps intercepting construction in tests) is now treated as "no override" rather than silently shadowing a registered backend for all 18 rate-limited destinations; a test's patched mock or a genuinely different caller-supplied factory still wins outright. **drt-core ships no distributed reference backend, Redis client, dependency, optional extra, or new config default.** `get_rate_limiter_backend()` also triggers plugin discovery itself, matching `drt/connectors/registry.py`'s `_ensure_plugins_loaded()` fix for the same class of gap: plugin registration used to happen only in the Typer CLI's root callback, so an installed `drt.rate_limiter_backends` entry point stayed silently unregistered under the MCP server, dagster-drt, and Airflow/Prefect's `run_drt_sync()` — exactly the orchestrator-launched entry points #921 is about. `load_plugins()` also gained a same-thread reentrancy guard: a registration callback that itself reads the currently active backend (a plausible "wrap the existing factory" pattern) re-enters `load_plugins()` while the outer call still holds its (non-reentrant) lock, reproduced directly as a genuine, indefinite hang; a `threading.local()` flag now lets a same-thread nested call return immediately while a different thread loading concurrently still blocks normally. The guide's example distributed backend derives an opaque HMAC digest from `key` rather than writing it verbatim into Redis, matching the existing "do not log or serialize" contract on `rate_limit_key()`. The zero-code default mitigation is documented too: batch changed syncs into one process with `--select state:modified --state <manifest>` (#772) instead of launching one process per sync. This closes #921 at its stated decision/documented-workaround bar, not by claiming separate processes now coordinate automatically. Docs: `docs/guides/rate-limiting.md`, `docs/guides/plugins.md`.

- **Third-party connector types are usable from a sync YAML** ([#997](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/997), [ADR 0009](docs/adr/0009-plugin-config-union-blocker.md)): #297 shipped entry-point discovery, but a connector registered that way could never be *named* — `SyncConfig.destination` was a closed pydantic union and `load_profile()` a closed `if source_type == ...` chain, both rejecting an unrecognized `type` before `drt/connectors/registry.py` was ever consulted. `pip install drt-salesforce-premium` registered a connector that no YAML could reference. `DestinationConfig` now uses a callable `Discriminator` with a `GenericDestinationConfig` catch-all, and `load_profile()` falls back to the registry after its built-in chain. The 34 built-in destination config classes are unchanged and still parse to their own concrete types, so `isinstance()` narrowing in `drt/destinations/query.py` and throughout `drt/destinations/` is unaffected. **Parse errors for built-ins are deliberately not weakened**: the discriminator routes to the catch-all only for a type the registry actually holds, so a typo'd built-in (`type: postgress`) still fails with the same `union_tag_invalid` error listing the expected types, rather than parsing as a plugin and failing much later at connector resolution — the constraint ADR 0009 named as the reason not to take this direction naively. A plugin that registers a `config_class` **subclassing `GenericDestinationConfig`** gets validated against it — its field types are enforced, its defaults apply, and its `describe()`/`rate_limit_key()` overrides run, so `assert isinstance(config, MyConfig)` in a connector's `load()` holds. Subclassing is what keeps the instance inside `SyncConfig.destination`'s declared union, so it serializes without pydantic's best-effort fallback and inherits `retry`/`rate_limit`. Registering anything else falls back to `GenericDestinationConfig` that carries fields verbatim; on that path drt-core refuses names it reads off a destination config itself (`lookups`, `table`, `upsert_key`, `model`, `mode`) rather than absorbing them into an AttributeError mid-run. `retry` and `rate_limit` are inherited so paced/retried writes work for plugin destinations, and `describe_safe()` renders type-only so arbitrary plugin fields can never leak into a hosted docs site ([#696](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/696)). `drt plugins list` no longer labels connectors "not yet usable in sync YAML", and `SyncConfig`'s generated JSON Schema enumerates the registered plugin types so `drt validate`'s schema pass and its pydantic pass agree. Fixes a latent gap found in the same audit: `rest_api` was registered as a source and present in the `ProfileConfig` union but had no branch in the dispatch chain, so `load_profile()` rejected it — it now has its own dispatch branch, in `save_profile()` as well as `load_profile()` so its free-form `auth` block stays on the `*_env`-only path and a literal token is refused rather than written to profiles.yml. Docs: `docs/guides/plugins.md`.

- **Measured I/O-vs-CPU profiling and a recommendation—not a Rust commitment** ([#301](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/301), [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-rust-migration-decision.md)): `make profile` wraps #280's unchanged 100 / 10,000 / 100,000-row `execute_scenario()` workloads with stdlib `cProfile`, attributes non-overlapping wall time to CPU-bound in-memory SQLite extraction, engine/record/JSON CPU, and destination filesystem work (including each `os.makedirs` `mkdir`/`stat` subtree as well as file open/write/flush), and writes schema-v1 JSON artifacts under ignored `benchmarks/profiles/`. The measured local SQLite `:memory:` → engine → JSONL shape is **CPU-majority, refuting the bare “I/O, not CPU” claim only for this deliberately I/O-light workload**: combined CPU is 91.88% / 86.41% / 84.46%, versus 8.12% / 13.59% / 15.54% local destination I/O. At 100,000 rows, `json.dumps` is 0.174138 s / 38.95% of total, in-memory source extraction is 0.125501 s / 28.07%, and the non-JSON transformation residual is 0.078008 s / 17.45%; that residual still includes work outside `engine/sync.py`, so a PyO3 engine rewrite cannot claim even that share as an expected speedup. ADR 0010 consequently recommends against using a benchmark with no source I/O and only local buffered-file destination I/O to justify a broad rewrite, names wide/heavy transform+serialization workloads as the plausible native-code case, calls for representative remote profiles next, and explicitly defers the final migration/roadmap call to the repository owner. Manual maintainer tooling only (`scripts/run_profiling.py`), not CI automation; tests assert execution/schema invariants, never environment-dependent timings.

- **ADR 0010 follow-up: real network I/O + a throwaway PyO3 prototype** ([#1008](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/1008)): answers #301's own named gap — its benchmark's source performed no genuine storage or network I/O. `benchmarks/profile_real_io.py` (manual-only, `make profile-real-io`, requires Docker) profiles `PostgresSource.extract()` against a real ephemeral `postgres:16-alpine` container over a real TCP connection, and `RestApiDestination.load()` against a controlled-latency HTTP server running in a genuinely separate OS process, at 0/10/50/200ms. Real Postgres reads spend the overwhelming majority of time inside the source boundary (connection, round trip, driver row materialization) rather than in code a Rust rewrite of `engine/sync.py` would touch — but, read carefully, this does not by itself isolate a CPU/IO split, since the pure-Python code this method *can* measure separately (the outer consumer loop) is not where drt's own conversion work happens. For REST, the split is built from the known injected latency rather than asking cProfile to separate CPU from a blocking call: per-request non-network-wait cost is not flat across latency settings (3.6ms/request at 0ms latency, rising to ~19-20ms/request at 200ms), so it demonstrably contains latency-correlated local-transport wait, not pure CPU — the 0ms run gives a defensible upper bound of roughly 3.6–4.8ms/request CPU cost, but this method cannot cleanly separate CPU from wait at nonzero latency, and the ADR states that limitation directly rather than over-reading it. The primary evidence for the "don't rewrite" recommendation is instead the PyO3 prototype: a scratch-only (not committed) `pyo3`/`serde_json` extension benchmarked head-to-head against `json.dumps`, including the full Python↔Rust boundary/conversion cost, showed a real but modest 1.2–1.6× speedup at small batches that flattens to statistical noise (0.95×–1.09× across three runs) at 100,000 records — the scale where a win would matter most. Neither experiment changes ADR 0010's recommendation against a broad `engine/sync.py` rewrite; see ADR 0010's "Follow-up" section for full detail, including a corrected-methodology note (an earlier draft of this section mislabeled network latency as CPU time and hit a same-process-threading measurement bug, both fixed before merge).

- **Reproducible local sync-performance benchmark suite** ([#280](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/280)): performance work toward the Rust-migration decision had no stable baseline — ad-hoc timings could quietly change the source, destination, batch shape, or row payload between measurements. `make benchmark` now runs fixed 100 / 10,000 / 100,000-row scenarios through the real `SQLiteSource` → `engine.run_sync()` → JSONL persistence path, entirely locally with no credentials, network service, or optional connector dependency.
