Each scenario records total wall-clock duration, rows/sec, `tracemalloc` peak Python memory, and real destination-call count as the API-call proxy in a versioned, JSON-Schema-documented file under ignored `benchmarks/results/`, stamped with UTC time and the full git commit. The unmeasured `execute_scenario()` seam is deliberately importable and separated from timing, memory, and serialization so #301 can wrap the identical workloads with cProfile/py-spy instead of rebuilding a subtly different microbenchmark. **Codex-review fixes:** the benchmark JSONL destination now appends after its first batch and verifies the persisted line count after measurement — the previous wrapper delegated to `FileDestination.load()`, whose per-call truncation left medium/large runs timing repeated 100-row overwrites despite reporting 10,000/100,000 successful rows — and the harness pins process-global OpenTelemetry to no-op providers before the first scenario, so an operator's OTLP environment/profile cannot add exporter initialization, background network activity, or first-scenario-only overhead. This remains manual maintainer tooling (`scripts/run_benchmarks.py`), not a shipped `drt benchmark` end-user command and not CI/release automation; automation is deferred until measured need exists. Docs: `benchmarks/README.md`.

- **dbt exposures export** ([#781](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/781)): `drt docs generate --format dbt-exposures` emits a dbt `exposures:` YAML block to stdout, one `type: application` / `maturity: high` exposure per sync whose model is an exact `ref(...)` and is not shadowed by a higher-precedence `syncs/models/<name>.sql` override. Each exposure depends on that dbt model, retains the original drt sync name, destination type, and mode under `meta.drt`, and uses a normalized, collision-safe dbt identifier. Its optional HTML link is computed deterministically relative to the root served by `dbt docs serve` (`docs/sync/*.html` for the default output), without probing the filesystem; links are omitted only for HTML page-slug collisions or an unservable `--output` outside `target/`. Output is sorted by sync name and carries no timestamp, so unchanged projects regenerate byte-identically and can use a committed exposure file as a CI drift gate. Raw-SQL and locally overridden syncs are skipped with deterministic YAML comments; drt does not parse SQL heuristically, write into the dbt project automatically, or push to the dbt Cloud API. Docs: `docs/guides/using-with-dbt.md`, with a generate-and-`git diff --exit-code` recipe in `docs/guides/ci-cd-integration.md`.

- **age encryption for project-local `.drt/secrets.toml`** ([#303](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/303)): new `drt encrypt <path>` / `drt decrypt <path>` commands backed by the optional `pyrage` binding (`pip install 'drt-core[encryption]'`), using an X25519 age identity from `DRT_SECRETS_KEY` and `.enc` output (`secrets.toml.enc`). Encryption preserves the original plaintext by default and says so explicitly; both directions refuse accidental overwrite unless `--force` is supplied, and plaintext writes are owner-only on POSIX. Every existing `secrets.toml` credential-resolution path now gives the encrypted sibling precedence and decrypts it entirely in memory, without a plaintext temp file. Missing, invalid, or non-matching keys fail with actionable messages instead of surfacing as TOML parse errors or silently falling back to stale plaintext. Two Codex-review fixes close real edge cases: Python 3.10 without the encryption extra now reports the missing TOML parser when an encrypted file exists instead of silently loading zero secrets, and no-overwrite writes now use atomic OS-level exclusive creation so concurrent encrypt/decrypt commands cannot race past the guard. Scope is intentionally limited to `secrets.toml`; state/history/DLQ files do not carry connector credentials and remain unchanged. Docs: [`docs/guides/secrets-encryption.md`](docs/guides/secrets-encryption.md).

- **Enterprise boundary design: `PermissionChecker` (RBAC, #298) and `AuditLogger` (audit hooks, #299)** — [ADR 0008](docs/adr/0008-enterprise-boundary-rbac-and-audit-hooks.md). Design-only, both issues' explicit scope: no enforcement logic, no event shipping. `drt.security.PermissionChecker` (`check(action, sync_name, principal=None)`, actions `run`/`edit`/`view`) is wired at three representative CLI hook points (`drt run`, `drt state reset`, `drt status`), all no-ops under the OSS default `AllowAllPermissionChecker` — `CLAUDE.md`'s "no RBAC" line stays true of OSS's actual behavior. Sync-lifecycle audit events (`sync_started`/`sync_completed`/`sync_failed`) reuse the existing `SyncObserver` Protocol via a new `register_extra_observer()` — no new Protocol, since `SyncObserver` already covers them. The two genuinely new events (`config_changed`, fired from both `load_syncs()` and `load_project()` so direct callers like `drt status` are covered too, not just the `drt run` path; `secret_accessed`, fired after `provider.fetch()` returns and after the secret-cache lock is released — audited outside the lock specifically so an Enterprise `AuditLogger` resolving its own sink credentials via the same resolver can't deadlock on it, caught in Codex review — never logging the resolved value) get a narrow new `drt.observability.AuditLogger` Protocol, no-op by default (`NoOpAuditLogger`). Both audit call sites catch and log `log_event()` failures rather than letting an unreachable audit sink break the config load or secret fetch it's auditing (also a Codex-review fix — the Protocol's documented best-effort contract needed the call site to enforce it, not just implementations to volunteer it). Registration mirrors `SecretProvider.register()`'s existing pattern exactly (`register_permission_checker`/`register_audit_logger`/`register_extra_observer`); how a third-party package's registration call gets triggered without an explicit operator-side import is the same open gap `SecretProvider` already has, left to #297 (plugin discovery) rather than solved here. Both new Protocols join [ADR 0007](docs/adr/0007-protocol-stability-policy.md)'s freeze-scope table, public and frozen at v1.0.

- **Entry-point plugin discovery (#297)** — `drt/plugins.py`, six `drt.*` entry-point groups (`drt.sources`, `drt.destinations`, `drt.secret_providers`, `drt.permission_checkers`, `drt.audit_loggers`, `drt.observers`), discovered and invoked once at CLI startup via `importlib.metadata.entry_points()`. Closes the exact gap #299's ADR 0008 named: `SecretProvider`/`PermissionChecker`/`AuditLogger`/extra-`SyncObserver` registration all previously required an explicit operator-side import; `pip install`ing a package exposing one of these four entry-point groups is now sufficient. A broken plugin's exception is caught and reported per-entry, not propagated — one bad third-party package can't take down the CLI. New `drt plugins list` command (`--format table|json`) shows what was discovered, including package/version/author metadata and per-entry load status. **`drt.sources`/`drt.destinations` shipped as a deliberate partial exception** in this PR: entry points in these two groups were discovered, invoked, and did register in `drt/connectors/registry.py`, but the resulting type could not yet be named in a sync YAML, because `SyncConfig.destination` and `load_profile()` both validated `type` against a closed, hand-enumerated set before the connector registry was consulted. **[#997](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/997), in this same release, closes that** — see its entry above; the `drt plugins list` label it describes is gone with it. See [ADR 0009](docs/adr/0009-plugin-config-union-blocker.md) for why, the candidate designs considered, and why closing it is scoped to [#997](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/997) rather than folded into this one. Docs: `docs/guides/plugins.md`.

### Changed

- **Replaced the hardcoded `_QUERYABLE_TYPES` config-class isinstance tuple with a `QueryableDestination` Protocol** ([#469](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/469)), backing `drt test`'s per-sync validation queries. Postgres/MySQL/ClickHouse/Snowflake now implement `get_table_name()`/`execute_test_query()` on themselves; a new SQL destination gains `drt test` support by implementing these two methods, with no edit to `drt/destinations/query.py` needed. Scoped to `drt test` only — `drt run --dry-run --diff`'s true-diff path and `drt test --store-failures` also call `query.py`'s `fetch_rows`/`fetch_rows_by_keys`/`fetch_failing_rows`, which remain separately, config-class-dispatched (their per-dialect coverage genuinely differs, e.g. ClickHouse has no keyed fetch — a single Protocol can't express that without erasing real distinctions); both already degrade gracefully rather than crash for an unsupported destination, same as before this Protocol existed. Purely internal otherwise — `is_queryable()`/`get_table_name()`/`execute_test_query()` keep their existing public signatures and behavior. Landed as a new, separate Protocol per [ADR 0007](docs/adr/0007-protocol-stability-policy.md)'s extension mechanism rather than the originally-proposed `Destination.fetch_existing()` — `Destination.load()` itself was never touched, so this carried no breaking-change risk. Closes [#300](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/300)'s companion issue; ADR 0007 also closes #300 itself (Protocol stability review and freeze preparation), see PRs [#992](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/992)/[#993](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/993).

## [0.9.1] - 2026-08-18

**Patch release for [#986](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/986) — tracked mirror on least-privilege MySQL users, plus the state-diff privilege reduction on Snowflake, and three other fixes accumulated since v0.9.0.**

### Fixed

- **Tracked mirror's state-diff step on least-privilege MySQL users broke the documented no-DDL contract** ([#986](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/986)). The SQL-side state diff introduced by #694 part 2 in v0.8.4 unconditionally issued `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` even when an admin had pre-provisioned `_drt_synced_keys`: MySQL requires the database-scoped `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLES` privilege for this (which cannot be granted only on the two sanctioned tables). This was a regression, not a destination-config error — #695 and the MySQL/Postgres connector guides explicitly promised that table-scoped grants were sufficient, while both v0.8.4 and v0.9.0 described the upgrade as non-breaking. Postgres/MySQL now attempt both staging-table creation and population inside one savepoint, roll it back on any failure (clearing Postgres's otherwise-poisoned transaction; harmless on MySQL), and compute both `previous − current` and `current − previous` from one client-side state read. The fast server-side path remains unchanged when staging succeeds, and the current incremental stale-delete/new-insert rewrite — including scoped-state preservation and backfill — is untouched. **Snowflake gets the identical state-diff fallback** (behind its established autocommit `try/except` guard, no savepoint needed), which is a real reduction in that one step's privilege floor — but ⚠️ **does not achieve no-DDL `sync.mode: mirror` on Snowflake**: its write path independently and unconditionally stages every mirror write through `MERGE` via its own `CREATE TEMP TABLE` ([#599](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/599), predates this regression by three releases), which needs the same schema-level `CREATE TABLE` regardless of `strategy`. Caught in Codex review on this PR; closing that gap is filed separately (see `docs/connectors/snowflake.md`). **Databricks was never part of this contract and needed no fix**: its mirror DELETE path (`_delete_via_staged_keys`) has unconditionally required `CREATE` on the target's catalog/schema since #707/#734 (v0.7.11), by design and documented in code — the #694 part 2 diff table adds no privilege this path didn't already require. **ClickHouse alone is genuinely unaffected**, binding current hashes as an `Array(String)` parameter with no staging table at all. Destination privilege failures now suggest checking database GRANTs for MySQL 1044/1142, PostgreSQL/Snowflake SQLSTATE `42501`, and Snowflake errno 3001 instead of the misleading URL/auth/headers hint. Real Postgres/MySQL `local_sql_smoke` coverage runs two tracked-mirror generations through users that explicitly lack temporary-table privilege and verifies the stale destination row is deleted.

- **Provider URIs (`aws-sm://`, `gcp-sm://`, `vault://`) silently failed on 12 connectors** ([#965](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/965)): the `discord`, `email_smtp`, `google_ads`, `google_sheets`, `intercom`, `jira`, `salesforce_bulk`, `slack`, `teams`, and `twilio` destinations, plus the `postgres` and `redshift` sources, read one or more `*_env` fields via plain `os.environ` access instead of `resolve_env()` (`drt/config/credentials.py`) — so a provider URI was treated as a literal environment-variable name, found nothing, and the connector failed auth as if the credential were unset. All twelve now route through `resolve_env()`, matching every other connector's existing pattern. Credential resolution also stays behind each destination's empty-batch short-circuit, preserving the no-network-I/O contract, and Jira resolves its provider-backed base URL once per batch rather than once per record. `docs/guides/secret-provider-uris.md` no longer carries the "not every connector" caveat.

- **`drt.integrations.airflow`/`prefect`'s `run_drt_sync()` never persisted state, on any backend.** `build_state_bundle()` (#756) correctly resolved `state.backend: local | gcs | s3`, but the resolved store was never wired into an observer — `run_sync()` falls back to a silent no-op observer whenever one isn't passed (`drt/engine/sync.py:296-297`) — so every save was a no-op regardless of backend, and watermark storage was never wired at all. Fixed by composing `StatePersistingObserver` (+ `DlqObserver` when `sync.dlq.enabled`) the same way the CLI's `_build_observer()` and `dagster-drt`'s `DagsterDrtResource.run()` already do. Found while researching [#855](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/855)/[#856](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/856) — the same bug class dagster-drt's fix closed, in the two integrations that fix didn't touch. `docs/guides/remote-state.md` corrected; it previously described this as "local-only", which understated it — nothing was persisted, local included.

- **`drt run --dry-run` (and every other dry-run path — `drt serve ?dry_run=true`, `DagsterDrtResource.run(dry_run=True)`, `run_drt_sync(dry_run=True)`) persisted an advanced cursor and a fake run record, even though nothing was sent to the destination** ([#978](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/978)). `new_cursor_value` is tracked from extracted rows regardless of `dry_run` (dry-run previews still extract), and `observer.on_sync_completed(...)` fired unconditionally — but `StatePersistingObserver` had no way to know the run was a preview, so it saved `last_run_at`/`records_synced`/`last_cursor_value` as if the sync had actually completed. A subsequent real run would then start from that advanced position and could silently skip the previewed-only rows. Root-caused rather than patched at each of the (currently three, and growing) call sites: `run_sync()` now sets a new `SyncResult.dry_run` field, so `StatePersistingObserver` (and any future persisting observer) can refuse to write on its own by reading `result.dry_run`, rather than relying on every caller to remember to omit it. Carried on `SyncResult` — a plain dataclass — rather than as a new `SyncObserver.on_sync_completed()` parameter: an earlier version of this fix added a Protocol parameter, which a Codex review caught as a breaking change for any `SyncObserver` implemented directly against the old five-argument signature (`drt-core`'s own four built-in observers were unaffected either way, but a library caller's custom observer would not have been). Existing behavior for real (non-dry-run) syncs is unchanged.

- **`drt.integrations.airflow`/`prefect`'s `run_drt_sync()` ignored `history.enabled: false`.** The `run_drt_sync()` state-persistence fix earlier in this changelog passed `history_manager=bundle.history` unconditionally — `run_sync()` appends history for every non-dry run whenever the manager is non-`None`, so a project explicitly disabling execution history via `history: {enabled: false}` in `drt_project.yml` still got it recorded through this path. Found via a Codex review on the identical fix for `dagster-drt`'s `DagsterDrtResource.run()`. Now matches the CLI's own `history_mgr = state_bundle.history if history_cfg.enabled else None` (`drt/cli/commands/run.py:758`): `history_manager=bundle.history if project.history.enabled else None`.

## [0.9.0] - 2026-08-11

### Added

- **REST API batch request bodies** ([#770](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/770)): `body_mode: batch` sends the engine's already-`sync.batch_size`-bounded record chunk in one HTTP request, or further splits it with `max_records_per_request`, replacing one-request-per-row activation against bulk endpoints without creating a second batching authority in the destination layer. `batch_template` renders with `rows=<post-mapping records>` and the existing `tojson_safe` filter handles the whole list; rate limiting is charged once per outer request, so the configured RPS now buys N records of throughput in batch mode, while retry still wraps the complete request atomically. Non-2xx responses fail the whole request chunk by default; optional dotted `error_path` maps an exactly length-matched, index-aligned JSON list where `null` means success and non-null entries become row errors (message priority `error` → `message` → `error_message`). A malformed mapping logs a warning and safely fails the whole sub-chunk rather than partially pairing the wrong records, and global `RowError.batch_index` offsets remain correct across multiple sub-chunks for DLQ handoff. `on_error: fail` stops after the first sub-chunk containing any failure; empty batches perform no rendering, limiter acquisition, or network I/O. Default `body_mode: record` is unchanged. Docs: `docs/connectors/rest-api.md`.
- **State-aware selection — run only the syncs this PR changed** ([#772](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/772)): CI no longer has to choose between running every sync and running nothing when a PR changes project configuration. `drt run --select state:modified --state ci-baseline/manifest.json --dry-run --diff` compares the current project with a prior `drt docs generate --format json` artifact; the same `--state` flag and `state:modified` / `state:new` selectors work on `drt build`, `drt test`, and `drt validate`. Manifest schema v3 adds `syncs[].config_hash`, and one shared resolver carries the resulting diff through selection without inventing a second state format. The hash covers sync-file content (plus referenced local model SQL), not resolved configuration: secrets referenced through environment variables structurally cannot enter it, while an environment-variable-only change is deliberately invisible — the same accepted blind spot dbt documents. A missing, unreadable, or malformed baseline warns and treats every current sync as new/modified, so the first CI run works rather than failing closed; a pre-v3 manifest instead exits clearly because it cannot contain comparable hashes. See [ADR 0006](docs/adr/0006-manifest-schema-v3.md).

- **`sync.computed_fields` — declarative derived columns** ([#763](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/763)): drt could rename a column (`field_mappings`) and obscure one (`mask`), but not *derive* one. The workarounds were to push destination-specific formatting into a shared dbt mart — one mart per destination, which is mart sprawl — or to use the REST destination's `body_template`, which the other 33 structured destinations do not have. Now `computed_fields: {name: "<jinja>"}` derives payload-shaped columns at the engine seam, so every destination gets them: `full_name: "{{ row.first }} {{ row.last }}"`, `phone_e164: "+81{{ row.phone | replace('-', '') }}"`, `source_system: "drt-${ENV}"`. Same Jinja environment, filters and `StrictUndefined` strictness as `body_template` — one templating story. **A single-expression template keeps the value's Python type** (`{{ row.n * 1000 }}` reaches a BIGINT column as `5000`, not `"5000"`), while anything with surrounding text renders as a string; Jinja's own `native_concat` is deliberately **not** used, because it `literal_eval`s the result and would turn a column holding the *string* `"123"` into the integer `123` while leaving `"01"` alone — the same config behaving differently row by row, decided by the data. **Ordering:** `computed_fields` → `field_mappings` → `mask`. Templates therefore read **source** column names, matching how `cursor_field` and `lookups` already read, and the rename and mask apply to what they produced. ⚠️ This is *not* the order sketched in the issue (`computed_fields` → `mask` → `field_mappings`), which would have inverted the shipped meaning of every existing `mask:` key — they reference post-rename names today, so a mask configured against the destination-facing name would have silently stopped matching and let PII through. **A computed field can never read another computed field**: every template is evaluated against the record as it arrived, so results do not depend on YAML key order — the same guarantee `field_mappings` makes, and the reason chaining was rejected rather than overlooked. Writing an existing column name *is* allowed and replaces it in place, for normalisation (`phone` → E.164) without inventing a second name. Template **syntax** is validated at config load — a malformed template stops `drt validate` instead of surfacing on the first row of a production run — while a missing column stays a run-time error governed by `on_error`: `skip` drops the row with a `RowError` (never half-derived: fields are assigned only once all of them succeeded), `fail` raises and names the field, since a template failure is nearly always one config defect affecting every row alike and reporting it once beats reporting N skipped rows and zero synced. Error handling deliberately catches **every** exception a template can raise, not just the missing-column one — `{{ row.a / row.b }}` over a zero raises `ZeroDivisionError` straight out of Jinja, and catching narrowly would let it abort a run that had explicitly asked to skip bad rows. ⚠️ One inherited Jinja behaviour to know: a NULL passed *through a filter* renders as the literal string `"None"` (`{{ row.phone | replace('-', '') }}` over a null phone), because Jinja stringifies before filtering; a bare `{{ row.phone }}` correctly stays null. This is shared with `body_template` and is not special-cased — write `{{ (row.phone or '') | replace('-', '') }}` (parens required: `|` binds tighter than `or`, so the unparenthesized form only applies the filter to `''`). Prior art: dlt's `add_map`, Census / Hightouch computed mapper fields; aggregations and cross-row logic remain out of scope (that is SQL's job).

- **`state:` project config block** ([#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756), step 1 of 4): builds on [#900](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/900)'s prior pure-interface extraction (`StateStore`/`HistoryStore`/`DlqBackend` Protocols + back-compat aliases, no call-site changes, paired with a set-equality test against each local implementation's public API so a Protocol can't silently stop covering a method — caught a near-miss where `StateStore.reset()` had arrived nine days after the Protocol's method list was written). This step adds a new `drt_project.yml` `state.backend` field (`local` only for now — this is groundwork, not a new capability) and a shared `build_state_bundle()` factory replacing 24 direct `StateManager`/`HistoryManager`/`DlqStore` constructions across the CLI/MCP/docs surfaces. Behaviourally a no-op — every call site still gets the same local file-backed stores it did before — but it's what makes a remote (GCS/S3) backend a config change instead of a rewrite in the follow-up PRs this stack sets up. The bundle is cached per project directory rather than constructed fresh per call: `drt run --threads N`'s existing per-instance locking only stays meaningful once every thread shares the same store instance, which matters more once that instance is a network client rather than a local file handle.
- **`state.backend: gcs`** ([#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756), step 2 of 4): the first working remote backend — `state.json`/history/DLQ each become one GCS object per sync (or one for state), read-modify-written under a generation precondition rather than last-write-wins, closing the same failure class as [#919](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/919) (a lost update between two writers that finish together) for drt's own state rather than just watermarks. Reads are pinned by a *versioned* blob reference rather than `if_generation_match` on download — the two are not equivalent: a competing writer landing between the metadata check and the download raises `404` on a versioned read but `412` on an `if_generation_match` download, and conflating the two would let a genuinely-present object's read be mistaken for "never existed", producing a wrong create-only write. Retries use jittered exponential backoff (up to 8 attempts — higher than a single watermark's 5, since `state.json` is shared by every `--threads N` worker rather than by two independent writers) and raise `StateContentionError` on exhaustion for state (silent loss is exactly the bug class this closes) while history/DLQ stay best-effort, matching local's existing swallow-and-log posture. Remote history is additionally capped by `history.max_entries` (default 500) on top of `retention_days`, bounding the object a conditional write reads and rewrites — a documented behaviour difference from local, which keeps everything inside the retention window with no count cap. The DLQ observer now buffers failed batches and flushes once per sync instead of writing on every failed batch, which also reduces local DLQ file writes. New `object_store_smoke` pytest marker (real `fake-gcs-server` via `testcontainers`, every PR, no secrets — same posture [#941](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/941) established for Postgres/MySQL) opens with a mandatory canary asserting the emulator actually enforces both preconditions before any concurrency test runs, so a permissive emulator fails loudly instead of reporting false-green results.
- **`state.backend: s3`** ([#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756), step 3 of 4): the same object-store primitive against Amazon S3, using ETag preconditions in place of GCS's generations. S3 has no equivalent of a generation-addressed read, so a stale snapshot surfaces as a `412` on the pinned `GetObject` (or `404` on a concurrent delete) rather than the versioned-read `404` GCS gets almost for free — the read path retries explicitly rather than relying on a single primitive doing the work. `PutObject` uses `IfNoneMatch: "*"` for create-only and `IfMatch: <etag>` for update-if-unchanged; an S3-compatible endpoint that can't honour a conditional header (e.g. `501`) fails loudly rather than silently falling back to an unconditional write. Credential resolution matches `S3Destination`'s existing shape exactly (`aws_access_key_id_env`/`aws_secret_access_key_env`/`aws_session_token_env`, `region`, `endpoint_url`) so state storage doesn't introduce a second AWS auth vocabulary. `boto3` floor bumped to `>=1.35.69` — the version that added conditional-write support to `PutObject`. Same `object_store_smoke` marker as the GCS leg, now covering both backends via LocalStack's S3 service, with its own three-part canary (create-only, stale-`IfMatch`-on-write, stale-`IfMatch`-on-read all independently verified enforced before any concurrency assertion runs).
- **Remote state operations and migration guide** ([#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756), step 4 of 4): documents GCS/S3 setup, authentication and scoped IAM, the shipped object layout and contention behavior, bidirectional migration from `.drt/`, and the independence of project state from per-sync watermark storage. The sync-history, DLQ, CI/CD, and LLM API references now point operators to the durable backend where local-only wording previously implied `.drt/` was the only location.

- **First-class `run_id` + `sync_run_id` correlation** ([#762](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/762), part 1 of 2): every `drt run` process now generates a `run_id` (one per process, threaded through every sync it executes), and every `run_sync()` call — including library callers that never pass one at all — generates its own `sync_run_id`. Both flow through `SyncResult`, `HistoryEntry`, `DeadLetter`, `--output json`, OTel span attributes, and failure/degraded alert context, all for free: `_run_sync_body` stamps both ids onto its `total_result` object immediately after construction, before any observer or alert dispatcher ever sees it, so no `SyncObserver` Protocol signature change was needed. Deliberately **not** reconciled with [#768](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/768)'s query-tagging `new_run_id()` (a short lowercase-hex id sized to fit SQL comments and warehouse label length limits) — forcing a full UUID4 through that path risked corrupting the exact budget it exists to respect. Plain `uuid.uuid4()`, not the issue's proposed `uuid7`: none of the six correlation surfaces above sort by id, and `uuid7` isn't in the stdlib on any supported Python (3.10–3.13). One documented gap: if `run_sync()` raises before ever returning a `SyncResult`, the CLI's `--output json` error entry for that one failure has no object to read `sync_run_id` from (the history entry and failure alert written inside the same `finally` block, before the re-raise, still get it correctly).
- **`sync.metadata_columns` — opt-in engine-injected bookkeeping columns** ([#762](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/762), part 2 of 2): drt wrote destination rows byte-for-byte from the source, with no marker of *when* a row synced, *which run* produced it, or *which sync* owns it — debugging a stale-looking CRM row meant cross-referencing local `.drt/history` files a CI runner throws away between runs. Now `metadata_columns: {synced_at: _drt_synced_at, run_id: _drt_run_id, sync_name: _drt_sync_name}` adds any subset of the three as plain dict enrichment at the engine seam, so every destination that takes a `list[dict]` gets them — SQL, SaaS, and file/blob alike — not just the REST destination. Prior art: dlt's `_dlt_load_id` + `_dlt_id`, dbt's `invocation_id`. **`synced_at`/`run_id` are computed once per `run_sync()` call and shared by every row it writes** (matching a load-id's "one per load" semantics), not recomputed per-record or per-batch; `run_id` is `null` for library callers that don't pass one to `run_sync()`, the same nullability part 1's `SyncResult.run_id` already has. **Ordering: last of every transform**, applied after `mask` — unlike `computed_fields` (which runs first): the column names here are already destination-facing (chosen directly in config, not derived from a source column) and the values are engine bookkeeping, not source data, so there's nothing for `field_mappings` to rename or `mask` to obscure. This also means a `computed_fields` template can never observe a metadata column, since it doesn't exist yet when `computed_fields` runs. Requires the target column to already exist on the destination — this is dict enrichment, not DDL; a destination without that column fails the write normally, governed by `on_error`. A record that lands in the DLQ carries its metadata columns from the *original* failed attempt: `drt retry` resends the stored record verbatim, so a successfully-retried row's `_drt_synced_at`/`_drt_run_id` trace back to when it first failed, not the retry — deliberate, not a gap. Docs: `docs/guides/metadata-columns.md`.

- **`sync.unit_tests` — fixture rows in, expected payload out, zero credentials** ([#780](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/780)): once `mask`, `field_mappings`, and destination lookups could all touch a record before it ships, the only feedback loop for "does this mapping do what I think" was `drt run --dry-run` against a real warehouse — slow, and unavailable to a contributor without cloud credentials or to CI on a pull request. `unit_tests:` runs `given` fixture rows through the **real** engine transform chain (`computed_fields` → `field_mappings` → `mask` → `metadata_columns`, via a `FakeSource` and a capturing destination) and compares the result against `expect` — no network call, no credential, no destination connection. `expect` is a **subset match per row** — only the keys a test declares are checked, so a sync's source columns growing later doesn't break every existing unit test — but row **count** is still checked exactly, since a transform that drops or duplicates a row is exactly what a unit test exists to catch. `drt test --unit`, `--output json`, `--select`, `--fail-fast`; wired into `drt build` and the `drt_run_test` MCP tool. **Not supported yet**: a sync whose destination has `lookups:` configured — lookups resolve against the real destination and there is no fake for that — reported as a failed test rather than a crash; mutually exclusive with `--dry-run` and `--store-failures`, which are destination-connected concepts unit tests don't have. `alerts` are stripped before the sync runs (a shallow `model_copy`, the caller's own config object untouched) — otherwise a test deliberately exercising a failure path would fire real Slack/webhook alerts from inside `run_sync()`'s own `finally` block, since alert dispatch there is not observer-mediated. Prior art: dbt unit tests (1.8+), Census/Hightouch mapper previews with sample records. Docs: `docs/guides/sync-unit-tests.md`.

- **Secret provider URIs — resolve credentials from AWS/GCP Secret Manager or Vault** ([#782](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/782)): production reverse ETL concentrates *write* credentials to CRMs, ad platforms, and warehouses — exactly the secrets an org keeps in a managed store with rotation policies attached, while the default path (export into an env var in every runner) duplicates secret-distribution logic per orchestrator and breaks rotation the moment it's exported. Any `*_env` field in a profile or destination config can now be a `scheme://...` URI instead of a plain env var name: `aws-sm://<secret-id-or-arn>#<key>` (`[aws-secrets]` extra), `gcp-sm://projects/<project>/secrets/<secret>/versions/<version>#<key>` (`[gcp-secrets]`, GCP always names a version explicitly — `latest` is a real resolvable alias, not an implicit default), and `vault://<mount>/data/<path>#<key>` (`[vault]`; `#<key>` is **required** here, unlike AWS/GCP, since a KV v2 payload is always a field map with no "the secret is just one string" case to fall back to). **Resolution chain**: explicit YAML value → OS environment variable → `.drt/secrets.toml` → provider URI, tried in that order, so a provider URI is automatically inert wherever a real env var or `secrets.toml` key already resolves. A resolved value is **cached by full URI with a 300s default TTL** (`DRT_SECRET_CACHE_TTL_SECONDS`, read on every lookup, so changing it takes effect without a restart; a non-positive value disables caching entirely) — this is what lets [`drt serve`](docs/guides/using-webhook-trigger.md), a long-lived process that re-enters credential resolution on every triggered sync, pick up a rotated secret within one TTL window instead of holding the value it resolved at process start indefinitely ([#929](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/929), caught and fixed before this shipped in any release). The Vault leg goes one step further for a related reason: `hvac.Client()` captures `VAULT_TOKEN` once at construction and never refreshes it, and Vault tokens are conventionally short-TTL, so the Vault *client* is rebuilt on every fetch rather than cached — avoiding every fetch failing outright once the token expires, in favour of the value-cache's own bounded staleness. Not secret writing or rotation management (drt only reads), not local-file encryption (`.drt/secrets.toml` is a separate, unrelated mechanism), not OAuth token brokering, and not yet a third-party plugin surface (the provider registry is structured for one, but nothing installs today). Docs: `docs/guides/secret-provider-uris.md`.

### Changed

- **Template rendering compiles once instead of per record** ([#763](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/763)): `render_template` built a fresh Jinja `Environment` and re-parsed the template on **every call**, and all five of its callers — HubSpot, Klaviyo, Mixpanel, Zendesk, Linear — call it per record. Two module-level environments and an LRU cache of compiled templates replace that. Behaviour is unchanged, including the exception surface; Jinja templates are safe to render concurrently, which matters because `drt run --threads N` now renders through one shared cache.

- **Scope-aware SQL diff for tracked+scoped mirror, closing the gap #694 part 2 left open** ([#890](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/890)): `strategy: tracked` + `scope` composed together still pulled the **whole** per-sync tracked-key state table into Python before filtering by scope in memory — #694 part 2's server-side `NOT EXISTS` diff landed, but the scope narrowing came after it, in Python. On real Postgres 16 with 500 scopes × 200 keys (100,000 tracked rows), a run touching one scope and dropping 5 of its keys returned **99,805 rows** to Python before this change; **5** after — roughly 20,000× fewer rows crossing the wire, and faster too (44.4ms → 6.6ms, best of 3). Fixed identically across all four SQL destinations that implement tracked+scoped mirror — Postgres/MySQL, Snowflake, ClickHouse, Databricks — each dialect's own diff query now applies the scope filter server-side instead of after the fact. Rows written before scope tracking existed (`scope_spec`/`scope_key` still `NULL`, un-backfilled) correctly fall through to the existing Python-side filter, unchanged from today's behaviour — verified with a mixed backfilled/un-backfilled table rather than assumed.

- **Internal — `drt_run_test` stopped re-implementing the test loop** ([#851](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/851), PR [#927](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/927) by [@Muawiya-contact](https://github.com/Muawiya-contact)): the MCP tool ran its own copy of connect → build query → execute → check → severity → error handling, the same loop `execute_tests_for_sync` has run for `drt test` and `drt build` since [#777](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/777). Two implementations of one rule is precisely what [#400](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/400) was, and [#836](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/836) had to add MCP-side severity tests to pin behaviour a comment was otherwise holding in place. `run_test()` is now selection plus the JSON envelope: it picks the syncs and delegates per sync, so a change to severity semantics — or to anything else in the loop — reaches the MCP tool by construction rather than by remembering to. **The returned shape is unchanged field-for-field**, including `status` / `results[].sync` / `tests[]` / `skipped` / `reason`, and every existing test passes unmodified. Two arguments carry the difference in surface: `json_mode=True` + `quiet=True`, because the shared function prints a test header and a line per test for the CLI and MCP speaks over stdio; and (at the time) `store_failures=False`, because [#779](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/779)'s samples are written to `.drt/test_failures/` and MCP exposed no way to read one back — [#870](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/870) below turns that into a real opt-in parameter once the returned `path` itself answers the objection. One user-visible edge: the skipped-sync `reason` in `drt test --output json` gains the trailing word `destinations` ("tests not supported for rest_api **destinations**"), matching the wording the same command has always *printed*, the wording `drt_run_test` has always returned, and the wording `drt build`'s `tests_skipped_reason` inherits.

- **The drift audit now checks that CLI options reach their MCP tool** ([#871](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/871), PR [#877](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/877) by [@Pawansingh3889](https://github.com/Pawansingh3889)): `scripts/check_drift.sh` audited *connectors* and *MCP tool existence*, so adding a connector was caught and adding a **flag** was not. That is why the v0.8.0 wave — `--limit`, `--fail-fast`, `--failed`, `--vars`, `--threads` — shipped without MCP parity and went unnoticed for three releases ([#870](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/870)). The release checklist already asked for this by hand (`drt-release-check.md`, Phase 0 item 12), and it was manually ticked and silently passed each time; asking a human to enumerate "new CLI capabilities" from memory across a thirty-PR release is exactly the task that degrades. New check 9 does the enumeration instead, via `scripts/check_cli_mcp_parity.py`: it walks the Typer command tree, reads each MCP tool's signature, and reports any behaviour-changing option with no corresponding parameter. Presentation flags (`--output`, `--verbose`, `--quiet`, `--log-format`, `--format`) and selection flags (`--select`, `--exclude`) are structurally excluded with the reason recorded, and renames are declared rather than guessed (`--diff` → `compute_diff`, `--sync` → `sync_name`, and `--no-state` → `include_state`, an inverted alias). A command that is **neither** mapped to a tool nor explicitly listed as deliberately unmapped is itself reported — so a new command forces a decision instead of being silently skipped, which is #870's failure mode one level up. The 11 gaps it found are recorded — and, as of #870 directly below, cleared — in `scripts/drift_baseline.txt`; two of them — `test --dry-run` and `test --store-failures-limit` — were found by the check rather than by the hand audit. ⚠️ **The check refuses to report success if introspection breaks.** Typer 0.27 vendors its own Click, so `TyperOption` does **not** subclass the installed `click.Option`; the obvious `isinstance` filter matches nothing, every command enumerates zero options, and a parity check written that way passes forever while the gap it exists to catch grows. Options are therefore identified by duck-typing on `param_type_name`, and a canary assertion exits 2 rather than 0 if known-present options stop enumerating. For the same reason the script imports no `click` at all — Typer vendors it, so standalone Click is absent from a core install and importing it would work locally and fail in CI. The drift workflow gained a Python setup + core `pip install -e .` step (checks 1–8 are pure bash), and `drt/cli/**`, `drt/mcp/tools/**` were added to its `paths:` filter — without those a new flag would only have been caught by the weekly cron, reintroducing the lag this fixes. Trigger design is unchanged: the audit stays post-merge + weekly and never comments on contributor PRs.

- **MCP parity closed: `--limit`/`--vars` on `drt_run_sync`, `--dry-run`/`--fail-fast`/`--store-failures[-limit]` on `drt_run_test`, `--check-connection`/`--strict` on `drt_validate`** ([#870](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/870)): all 11 gaps `scripts/check_cli_mcp_parity.py` ([#871](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/871)) found are now closed or recorded as deliberate. `drt_run_sync` gains `limit` (→ `extract_limit`, same mirror/replace rejection as the CLI — a sample would delete or replace real rows) and `vars` (already a parsed dict, not the CLI's `'k: v, k2: v2'` string — resolved through the same `vars:` block < `DRT_VAR_*` < caller precedence via `project_vars()`, reused rather than reimplemented). `drt_run_test` gains `dry_run`, `fail_fast`, `store_failures`, and `store_failures_limit` — the multi-sync `--fail-fast` loop moved out of `drt test` into a new shared `run_test_suite()` (same one-implementation principle #851 above already applied to the per-test loop, applied one level up to the per-sync loop, and to the `unit=True` path too via `run_unit_test_suite()` once review caught `fail_fast` silently not reaching it there) so every surface schedules syncs identically. `store_failures` reverses #851's earlier "MCP can't read a sample back" reasoning: the returned `failures_stored.path` is a filesystem path under the project directory, and an MCP client with its own file access (the common case — an agent driving drt via MCP usually also has Read/Bash on the same checkout) can open it directly; MCP itself still doesn't read the file, it just stops pretending the path is useless. `drt_validate` — previously four lines returning only `valid`/`errors`, with no secret scan at all — now also runs the hardcoded-secret scan and reports `warnings` per sync (including for syncs that failed schema validation, matching the CLI), gains `strict` (promotes those warnings to `errors`, matching `drt validate --strict`) and `check_connection` (adds a `connection_tests` dict via the same `_run_connection_test` the CLI uses, keyed by sync name). **`run:--threads`/`--fail-fast`/`--failed` are deliberately excluded, not implemented**: `drt_run_sync` runs exactly one sync per call, and all three are multi-sync scheduling/selection concepts — parallelism across syncs, stopping *remaining* scheduled syncs, and a status-based selector with the same grammar `SELECTION_OPTIONS` already excludes `--select`/`--exclude` under ("an agent filters via `drt_get_status()` and calls `drt_run_sync` per name"). Recorded in `COMMAND_EXCLUSIONS["run"]` with each reason rather than left as baseline debt, since extending a single-sync tool with a second scheduling authority would contradict the selection precedent already shipped. `scripts/drift_baseline.txt`'s `cli-mcp:` section is now empty.

- **`drt serve` gets a real delivery contract** ([#854](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/854), ADR 0004 Tier 3, PR [#902](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/902) by [@Pawansingh3889](https://github.com/Pawansingh3889)): at webhook cadence the old endpoint had three defects that compounded — a second trigger was answered `423 Locked` and **dropped** (the sender believed it delivered; nothing ran — silent event loss, not backpressure), the sync ran synchronously inside the request handler (any sender with a delivery timeout recorded a failure for a sync that succeeded, then retried it), and the lock was global across *every* sync, so unrelated syncs queued behind each other. All three are replaced by one explicit promise, documented in the endpoint docs as a contract rather than an implementation detail: **a trigger accepted with `202` will run** — different syncs run concurrently, and same-sync triggers **coalesce to depth 1** (while a sync runs, the first new trigger creates one pending run; further triggers get the *same* run id with `"coalesced": true`, a flag that means "your trigger did not start a run of its own", covering both the trigger that creates the pending run and every one that joins it). Coalescing rather than a deeper queue is deliberate: drt syncs are watermark-driven, so one pending run picks up everything that accumulated — a queue of N would do the same work as a queue of 1. That reasoning holds *because* triggers are parameterless; if a trigger ever selects rows, the coalescing decision must be revisited. Dry-run and real triggers coalesce separately — a real trigger is never folded into a dry preview. ⚠️ **Two behaviour changes**: `423` is gone (a concurrent trigger now coalesces instead of dropping), and `POST /sync/<name>` now returns **`202` + a run id immediately** instead of holding the request open — poll `GET /runs/<id>` for the outcome, or pass **`?wait=true`** for the old synchronous `200`/`207` semantics (a `wait=true` request against a busy sync coalesces and blocks until the pending run completes). Run ids are **in-memory, valid for the lifetime of the `drt serve` process** — deliberately not persisted, because durable run ids are [#762](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/762)'s decision to make; when that lands, only the id's durability changes, not this contract. Auth became **pluggable** (`--auth none|bearer|hmac`; `auto` keeps the historical default of bearer-if-token-set): the bearer comparison now uses `secrets.compare_digest` (it was `==`), and new **HMAC-SHA256 body-signature verification** covers GitHub (`sha256=<hex>`, default header `X-Hub-Signature-256`), Shopify (base64, `--hmac-header X-Shopify-Hmac-Sha256`) and bare-hex senders. Stripe's timestamped scheme and Pub/Sub's OIDC JWT are *not* covered — OIDC is a filed follow-up ([#903](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/903) — it needs a JWT dependency decision), and until it lands Pub/Sub push still needs a verifying proxy. **Auth covers `GET /runs/<id>`, not just the `POST`**: that endpoint returns the full `SyncResult` and any error text, and while the run id is a uuid4 it rides in the URL path, so it reaches every reverse-proxy access log in front of drt. Leaving it as an implicit capability URL would have made the read path quietly weaker than the write path, in the one change whose stated purpose is making the contract explicit. `GET /health` stays open so load-balancer probes need no credential. Under `hmac` a `GET` has no body to sign, so its signature is over the **empty** body: a constant per secret rather than a per-request signature, documented in the guide together with the `openssl` one-liner that computes it and with when to prefer `bearer` for the polling path. One correctness fix underneath: `run_drt_sync()` gained an optional `state_manager` param and the server passes a **shared** state store, since `LocalStateManager`'s thread-safety is an instance lock and the previous per-request instances would have raced load-modify-save on `state.json` the moment different syncs ran concurrently. That param is typed as the **`StateStore` Protocol** ([#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756)) rather than the concrete local class, so `drt serve` is not the one caller structurally unable to take a remote state backend when those land. Request bodies are capped at 1 MiB (`413`); the run registry keeps the last 1000 finished runs, evicted by `finished_at` rather than by membership of a fixed set of state strings, so a status the runner's result contract grows later cannot leave a run permanently un-evictable. Docs: `docs/guides/using-webhook-trigger.md` (the contract, status-code table, per-scheme auth setup), `docs/llm/CONTEXT.md`.

### Fixed

- **`drt retry` silently dropped dead letters appended by a concurrent `drt run`** ([#955](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/955)). `replay_dead_letters()` read the DLQ once at the top, decided what to keep/drop against that snapshot, and wrote the result back at the end with `DlqBackend.replace()` — an unconditional whole-queue overwrite. A dead letter a concurrent `drt run` appended between that read and that write was never seen by the snapshot and was silently lost on the overwrite. Same failure class as [#919](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/919) (concurrent writers, a stale snapshot committed as truth), caught three days after that fix shipped. `DeadLetter` gains a stable `id` (assigned once at creation, preserved by `drt retry` across attempts — a retried-and-failed-again entry keeps its identity; only `attempts`/`timestamp`/`error_message` change) and both backends gain `DlqBackend.reconcile(remove_ids=..., updates=...)`, which re-reads current state itself and touches only the named entries — anything the caller never saw (a concurrent append, or an entry beyond `--limit`) survives untouched. `replay_dead_letters()` now calls this instead of `replace()`; `--limit`'s previously-explicit `untouched` bookkeeping is now implicit, since reconcile only ever names what it actually processed. **`ObjectStoreDlqBackend.reconcile()` is genuinely cross-process-safe** — the same generation/ETag preconditioning `append()` already used forces a retry against fresh state on a losing race, exhausting attempts raises rather than silently committing stale content. **`LocalDlqStore.reconcile()` is not** — it has no OS-level file lock (only a process-local `threading.Lock`, the same limitation its class docstring already documented), so a genuinely separate `drt run` **process** racing the read/write window still loses that append exactly as `replace()` did; what this fix does close for local is reconciling by identity from a fresh read rather than a stale in-memory snapshot, which matters the moment real file locking lands (a wholesale-overwrite operation could never be made cross-process-safe regardless of locking). Real local cross-process safety is filed separately as [#963](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/963), covering `LocalStateManager`/`LocalHistoryManager` too, not just this class. `--clear` still uses `replace([])` — a full discard racing a concurrent append can still lose that append on either backend, which is the documented "records are unrecoverable" contract, not a gap this fix left open. A retried entry's `sync_run_id` now correctly carries over from the original failure (previously reset to `null` on every retry — a byproduct fix, since the reconstructed `DeadLetter` object needed rebuilding here regardless), matching [#762](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/762)'s stated correlation-id semantics: a successfully-retried row traces back to when it first failed, not the retry. Entries written before `id` existed decode via a content-hash of their own JSONL line rather than a fresh random id per read — `replay_dead_letters()` reads the queue twice per invocation (once to decide what to retry, again inside `reconcile()`), and a random per-read id would make every legacy entry's remove/update silently never match, leaving it stuck in the queue and risking a duplicate resend on the next retry (caught in review).

- **Concurrent GCS watermark writers silently discarded each other's updates** ([#919](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/919), PR [#935](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/935) by [@Pawansingh3889](https://github.com/Pawansingh3889)). `GCSWatermarkStorage.save()` loaded the whole watermarks blob, set one key on the resulting dict, and uploaded the whole blob back with no precondition. Two writes that overlapped therefore both read the same base object and the later upload won outright, dropping the other sync's watermark with no error anywhere. That sync then re-read from a stale cursor on its next run and re-processed rows it had already synced, which is silent duplicate work against any destination that is not naturally idempotent. `delete()` had the same shape. Both now use the standard optimistic concurrency loop: read the object's generation, apply the change, and upload with `if_generation_match`, so a write that lost its race fails with a 412 and is retried against freshly read state instead of overwriting it. The download is pinned to the generation just read, so the bytes and the precondition token cannot come from different versions of the object, and a first write to a bucket with no object yet asserts `if_generation_match=0` ("only if still absent"). That read is itself two round trips, so it sits inside the retry boundary rather than before it: a competing writer landing between the `reload()` and the pinned download fails the download's own precondition, and because the object still exists that is a 412 rather than the 404 the read guards against. Caught at the same boundary as a lost upload, it costs one attempt and re-reads, instead of escaping `save()`/`delete()` as an unhandled `PreconditionFailed`, which is the one outcome `WatermarkContentionError` exists to avoid. Retries are bounded at 5 and exhausting them raises `WatermarkContentionError` rather than returning quietly, since a write that gives up in silence is the same failure this fixes. `get()` is unchanged, being a plain read with nothing to be conditional about. `BigQueryWatermarkStorage` was never affected: its `save()` is a `MERGE`, which the SQL engine already applies atomically per row. ⚠️ The write path now needs `google-api-core` (already a transitive dependency of the `[gcs]` extra) for its precondition exception types, resolved through the same lazy import the GCS client uses so a core install is unaffected. Found while reviewing [ADR 0005](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/pull/899), whose write-amplification note describes this same read-modify-write shape, so the approach here is a precedent for the remote state backend in [#756](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/756).

- **Databricks mirror DELETE on a composite `upsert_key`** ([#908](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/908)). Also shipped in [0.8.5](#085---2026-08-05).

- ⚠️ **The v0.8.4 tracked-mirror temp-table privilege regression was still present in v0.9.0 despite the no-DDL/drop-in-upgrade contract.** Fixed for Postgres/MySQL (a genuine no-DDL restoration) and Snowflake's state-diff step (a real privilege reduction, but not full no-DDL — see below) after this release in [#986](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/986); see [Unreleased](#unreleased).

## [0.8.5] - 2026-08-05

**Patch release for [#908](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/908) — Databricks mirror with a composite `upsert_key`.** Contains only that fix on top of v0.8.4.

### Fixed

- **`sync.mode: mirror` on Databricks failed at finalize whenever `destination.upsert_key` had more than one column** ([#908](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/908)). Delta rejects `(a, b) IN (SELECT a, b FROM staging)` with `DELTA_UNSUPPORTED_MULTI_COL_IN_PREDICATE`, so the mirror DELETE threw — **after the upsert had already been written**, leaving the run half-applied. Present since the staging anti-join landed in [#707](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/707) (v0.7.11) and shipped in every release since. Both mirror paths were affected, and **`mirror.scope` + `strategy: tracked` was hit unconditionally**: `scope` must be a subset of `upsert_key`, so any meaningful scoped configuration has a composite key — tracked+scoped mirror on Databricks had therefore never worked. Composite keys now go through `MERGE`, Delta's supported form for a multi-column anti-join delete (`WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE THEN DELETE` for plain/scoped, `WHEN MATCHED THEN DELETE` for tracked); a composite **`scope`** had the same defect independently — `(a, b) IN ((?, ?), …)` is the same rejected construct — and is now expanded to OR-of-ANDs. The single-column path is deliberately unchanged: it works, has shipped since #707, and its tests assert it binds no key parameters at all. ⚠️ `WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE` requires Databricks Runtime 12.2+. **Why four releases missed it:** the unit suite asserts the SQL drt *emits* and a mock cursor accepts any string, so it could only ever confirm drt was consistently generating invalid SQL — one test was even named `..._uses_tuple_form`, pinning the rejected construct verbatim. The live smoke harness had only ever exercised a **single-column** `upsert_key`, and had no `strategy: tracked` or `mirror.scope` coverage at all on any warehouse. Both gaps are now closed: `test_databricks_mirror_composite_upsert_key` reproduces the bug with the smallest configuration that triggers it, and is verified against the real smoke workspace rather than a mock.

## [0.8.4] - 2026-08-03

**Theme: the warehouse legs go fast, safe, and fully symmetrical.** Every SQL destination that implements `sync.mode: mirror` now supports both `strategy: tracked` and `scope`, and composes the two ([#692](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/692) / [#694](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/694) part 1) — Snowflake, ClickHouse, and Databricks catch up to the semantics Postgres/MySQL already had. Tracked mirror's own state diff moves from a full Python-side read to a server-side SQL join ([#694](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/694) part 2, **closing #694**) — a state table with millions of tracked keys is no longer read wholesale just to compute a typically-tiny diff, on every dialect that supports tracked mirror. **Query tagging** ([#768](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/768)) puts a `sync`/`run_id` SQL comment — plus native BigQuery/Snowflake/Databricks tagging — on every query drt issues, read or write, closing the "what does reverse ETL cost us" gap. On the extraction side, every SQL source now **streams** instead of buffering the whole result set ([#765](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/765)) and **retries** transient connect/execute failures ([#766](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/766)); **rate limiting** is now shareable across `--threads` and settable per destination ([#769](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/769)). And drt finally has a sanctioned way to **reset its own state** — `--full-refresh` plus `drt state show`/`reset`, with the destination-mutating `--tracked-mirror` level named explicitly rather than folded into a blanket `--all` ([#776](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/776)). No breaking changes — drop-in upgrade from v0.8.3.

### Added

- **`drt run --full-refresh` + `drt state show` / `drt state reset`** ([#776](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/776)): a sanctioned way to reset drt's durable state. Until now users hand-edited `.drt/*.json` — which does nothing when a remote watermark backend (GCS / BigQuery) is configured, and nothing at all for the tracked-mirror key table that lives *in the destination*. `--cursor-value` overrides one run's predicate but leaves stored state untouched: a probe, not a reset. drt keeps **three** kinds of durable state and each is now cleared separately: the watermark, the run state (`.drt/state.json`), and `_drt_synced_keys`. **There is deliberately no `--all`** — dlt reached the same conclusion with `drop_data`/`drop_resources`/`drop_sources`, and here the asymmetry is sharper: ⚠️ **`--tracked-mirror` re-baselines the destination**, after which rows written by the application (or another pipeline) count as drt's own and become deletion candidates on the next mirror pass — the exact scenario tracked mirror ([#686](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/686)) exists to prevent. So it must be named explicitly, and **`--full-refresh` is watermark-only**, matching the "re-read everything" meaning dbt users arrive with. `--full-refresh` is mutually exclusive with `--cursor-value` (one says "start from nothing", the other "start from here"). Both watermark sources are cleared together, which is subtler than it sounds: the engine reads `watermark_storage` first and `StateManager.last_cursor_value` only when no storage is configured (an `elif`), so clearing one alone would be silently ineffective in the other configuration — and "no watermark backend" is the default. Guards: `state reset` with no level exits 2 rather than assuming all of them, and refuses in a non-interactive context without `--yes`. **MCP parity ships with it** — `drt_state_show`, `drt_state_reset`, and `full_refresh` on `drt_run_sync` — rather than being retrofitted later ([#870](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/870) found the v0.8.0 flag wave never reached MCP at all). Out of scope, deliberately: destination **data** deletion, DLQ purge (`drt retry --clear`), history pruning. Docs: `docs/llm/API_REFERENCE.md`; skills: `/drt-debug`, `/drt-troubleshoot`.

### Changed

- **Destructive CLI commands now explain themselves in CI** ([#776](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/776)): `drt profile remove` previously called `typer.confirm` directly, so in a non-interactive context it printed a `[y/N]` prompt nobody could answer and aborted with a bare `Aborted.` — it failed correctly, but named neither the cause nor the fix, and the `[y/N]` actively suggested piping `y` would work when the answer is `--yes`. Both it and the new `drt state reset` now share one helper that says exactly that. Detection is by EOF rather than `isatty()`: a pipe carrying a real answer is not a TTY either, so an `isatty` check would refuse input that was genuinely supplied.

### Changed

- **Delta Lake source pushes filters and column selection into the scan** ([#679](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/679)): the source registered `to_pyarrow_table()` — the *entire* table materialised in memory before DuckDB ever saw the query — so a model's incremental `WHERE` reduced nothing and a large table risked an OOM however selective the query was. It now registers the lazy `to_pyarrow_dataset()`, so DuckDB pushes the predicate and the column list into the Parquet scan. Measured on a 300k-row table (~200B rows), fresh process, selecting 2 of 3 columns and ~1/9th of the rows: **+244 MB RSS before, +120 MB after**. The result set is no longer materialised either (batched, per [#765](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/765)). ⚠️ **Iceberg is unchanged and stays materialising** — pyiceberg exposes only `to_arrow()` (a full read) or `to_arrow_batch_reader()`, and the batch reader is **single-pass**: DuckDB drains it on the first scan, so a query that reads the table twice returns *silently wrong results* rather than an error (a self-join over 100 rows returns `0`). Correctness over memory; the Iceberg leg needs a lazy dataset equivalent that pyiceberg does not yet provide.

### Fixed

- **Databricks: authentication failures are no longer retried** ([#766](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/766) follow-up): a 401/403 from a bad or expired access token was retried three times on the extract path. The driver's own retry policy already classes these as hopeless — `auth/retry.py` answers 401 with *"Confirm your authentication credentials"* and 403 with *"403 codes are not retried"* — but it then surfaces them as `RequestError`, which **subclasses `OperationalError`**, so drt's transient check let them straight back in and retried what the driver had just given up on. Three rapid attempts with a bad token is the shape that trips a workspace lockout or an SSO alert, so this is the same hazard already excluded on Postgres/Redshift (SQLSTATE 28), MySQL (errno 1045) and SQL Server (error 18456). Now keyed on the HTTP status in `RequestError.context`, leaving the **warehouse cold start untouched** — a resuming warehouse raises `RequestError` with no status (or a 5xx), and that remains the single most valuable retry in this source ([#654](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/654)). The test double is also corrected: it had `RequestError` inheriting from `Error` rather than `OperationalError`, and that one wrong base is precisely what hid the bug from a green unit suite — a 401 never reached the check it needed to fail. A new test pins the fake against the real driver whenever the extra is installed.

- ⚠️ **#694 part 2 introduced an undeclared temporary-table DDL requirement for tracked mirror's state-diff step in this release**, contradicting #695's no-DDL pre-provisioning contract and the drop-in-upgrade claim above. Fixed for Postgres/MySQL (fully) and Snowflake (this step only — Snowflake's separate `MERGE`-staging write path still requires `CREATE TABLE`, see [Unreleased](#unreleased)) in [#986](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/986).

### Added

- **Streaming extraction for Postgres and Redshift** ([#765](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/765)): both sources read through a **server-side (named) cursor** in `fetch_size` batches instead of materialising the whole result set with `fetchall()`, so peak memory tracks the batch rather than the table. Measured on Postgres 16 with 300k rows of ~200B: **+196 MB RSS before, +16 MB after** (every figure in this entry is drawn from [`docs/research/extraction-memory.md`](docs/research/extraction-memory.md), the single source for them). A few-GB unload on a 7 GB CI runner was an OOM kill; now it is flat. New optional `fetch_size` profile field (default **10000**) — there is deliberately no value that restores the old behaviour, since buffering everything client-side is what this removes. Memory scales with `fetch_size x row width`, not row count, so lower it for very wide rows rather than for large tables. The `Iterator[dict]` contract is unchanged — this is entirely inside each source. ⚠️ **The connection is now held open for the whole load**, not just the extract: a server-side cursor dies with its session, so syncs are more exposed to idle-session reapers, WLM queue timeouts and failovers mid-load — and per [#766](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/766) a failure after the first row has been yielded is still not retried. Three lifecycle traps are handled and pinned by tests, each invisible to a mocked suite and found only against a live server: `cur.description` is `None` until the first batch arrives (a named cursor has not touched the server at `DECLARE` time, so reading columns after `execute()` raises `TypeError`); an abandoned generator (`--limit` / `--fail-fast`, [#775](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/775)/[#774](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/774)) leaks a server-side cursor unless the yield loop closes the connection in a `finally` that catches `GeneratorExit`; and Redshift's `SET search_path` must run on a *plain* cursor, since `DECLARE <name> CURSOR FOR SET` is a syntax error. **MySQL** streams too, via pymysql's unbuffered `SSCursor` — measured on MySQL 8 with the same 300k x ~200B shape: **+112 MB before, +3 MB after**. MySQL takes no `fetch_size`: `SSCursor` already streams row by row, and `fetchmany(n)` only calls `read_next()` n times rather than changing the number of round trips, so the knob would offer nothing but a way to use more memory (measured: iterating +3.6 MB, `fetchmany(100000)` +61.9 MB, no speed difference). Its `description` is populated right after `execute()`, unlike a psycopg2 named cursor, so columns are read up front. One MySQL-only hazard: **`cursor.close()` must run before `conn.close()`** — closing the connection under an unread `SSCursor` makes pymysql read from a socket it has already dropped and floods stderr with `AttributeError` via `Exception ignored in` (3 tracebacks per abandoned stream, measured). Correctness is unaffected, but for a CLI whose output people pipe that is its own breakage. **ClickHouse** streams via `query_rows_stream()` instead of `client.query()`, which materialised every row into `result.result_rows` first: **+224 MB → +149 MB** on the same 300k x ~200B shape. That ~1.5x is deliberately reported as-is rather than dressed up — it is much smaller than the Postgres (12x) and MySQL (37x) legs, because the remainder is clickhouse-connect buffering the HTTP response internally rather than drt holding rows. `max_block_size` does not move it (measured identical at 8192, 65536 and the default), so ClickHouse takes no `fetch_size` either: there would be nothing for it to control. **Snowflake and Databricks** stream too, completing the warehouse set. Snowflake iterates its cursor in `fetch_size` batches (it honours `arraysize`, so the shape matches the Postgres leg); Databricks iterates its cursor, whose `__iter__` delegates to a result set that is itself a `fetchone()` loop. Databricks takes **no `fetch_size`** — its cursor exposes no `arraysize` and `fetchmany(size)` requires an explicit argument, so there would be nothing for the field to set. Neither has a local server to measure against, so both gained **source-side legs in the dwh-smoke harness** ([#654](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/654)) — until now that harness drove both warehouses only as *destinations*, with DuckDB always the source, so the extract path had no real-service coverage at all. The new legs extract 50k generated rows and assert exact count, first/last row and no duplicates (a batch-boundary bug shows up as a short read or a repeat), plus an empty result and a mid-stream abandonment that would hang rather than fail if the generator's `finally` did not run. They skip without `DRT_SMOKE_*` credentials, so forks and local runs are unaffected. **SQL Server, DuckDB and SQLite** complete the set. SQL Server iterates its cursor in `fetch_size` batches (**+151 MB → +39 MB** through `extract()` on SQL Server 2022, 300k x ~200B). **The local-file sources were not exempt after all** — the initial read of #765 assumed "it's a local file, so buffering is free", and measuring disproved it: what the change removes is holding every row as a Python object, a cost a local file pays exactly like a remote warehouse. DuckDB **+150.9 MB → +42.2 MB**, SQLite **+110.6 MB → +4.4 MB**. DuckDB needs an explicit `fetchmany` loop (its result object has no `__iter__`, unlike every other driver here); SQLite and SQL Server iterate the cursor with `arraysize` set. ⚠️ **One deliberate semantic weakening:** SQL Server previously materialised the result set *inside* the retried closure, so a driver failing part-way through producing rows failed before anything was yielded and could be safely retried. That made it stricter than every other SQL source as an accident of buffering. Streaming removes the accident: a mid-stream failure now escapes with earlier rows already loaded, which is the documented [#766](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/766) boundary everywhere else. Pinned by a test so the old guarantee is not assumed to still hold. Docs: `docs/research/extraction-memory.md`, `docs/llm/API_REFERENCE.md`, `docs/connectors/{postgres,redshift}.md`.
- **Source-side retry for transient extract failures** ([#766](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/766)): a Snowflake session token expiring mid-extract, or a Databricks SQL warehouse cold-starting from auto-stop, used to fail the **entire sync** — even though the query is a pure read that is safe to repeat. Both were observed during the [#654](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/654) real-warehouse smoke programme, not hypothesised. The read is now retried. The load side has had retry since [#277](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/277); the extract side had none, so a single 503 on page 40 of a paginated REST read, or a Postgres connection reset, was terminal. Covers the **REST API source** (retryable status codes + transport errors per page request, `Retry-After` honoured via [#769](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/769)) and **seven SQL sources** — Postgres, Redshift, MySQL, Snowflake, Databricks, SQL Server, ClickHouse — each classifying its own driver's exceptions, since every driver is an optional extra no shared module can import. Classification is a **predicate, not a tuple of exception types**, because transient-ness frequently depends on an attribute rather than a class: Snowflake's `390114` token expiry is a `DatabaseError` and so are plenty of permanent errors; pymysql overloads `OperationalError` across both connection-lost errnos (2002/2003/2006/2013/2055) and permanent ones (1045 access denied, 1049 unknown database). Retried: connection-level failures (refused, server closed the connection, failover, a paused Redshift serverless workgroup resuming, a Databricks `RequestError` cold start). Not retried: `ProgrammingError` / `DataError` / `IntegrityError` — matched by specific class rather than the shared PEP 249 `DatabaseError` base, so a SQL typo fails fast instead of three times. **Authentication failures are never retried** on Postgres / Redshift / MySQL / SQL Server even though those drivers file them under an otherwise-retryable class (psycopg2 puts `InvalidPassword` under `OperationalError`; pymysql puts errno 1045 there; pymssql's `connect()` funnels *every* server exception into `OperationalError`, error 18456 "Login failed for user" included) — three rapid attempts with a bad credential can trip an account lockout, turning a config typo into an outage. ⚠️ **Scope boundary: retries cover establishing the connection, executing the query, and fetching the result set — a failure after the first row has been yielded is *not* retried and propagates.** The engine has already handed those rows to the destination and loaded rows cannot be un-sent; re-running would duplicate them, and skipping them would need a stable ordering and offset the query does not promise. That is a checkpointing problem, not a retry problem, and is deliberately out of scope. Deliberately excluded sources: **DuckDB / SQLite** (local files — "transient" isn't a meaningful category), **BigQuery** (`google-cloud-bigquery` already retries internally; a second layer would multiply the backoff), and **Delta Lake / Iceberg** ([#679](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/679)'s path). No configuration and no new config surface — `extract()` receives a `ProfileConfig`, not `SyncOptions`, so `sync.retry` is unreachable from inside a source; the `RetryConfig` defaults (3 attempts, 1s initial, ×2, 60s cap) ship the resilience and a per-profile knob is a deliberate follow-up. `with_retry` grew an opt-in `retry_on` predicate to make this possible; with it unset — every one of the 20 existing destination call sites — behaviour is unchanged. Docs: `docs/llm/API_REFERENCE.md`, `docs/connectors/{postgres,redshift,mysql,snowflake,databricks,sqlserver,clickhouse,rest-api}.md`.
- **Destination-level `rate_limit`, shared per endpoint** ([#769](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/769)): `drt run --threads 4` against one HubSpot portal was putting **4x the configured rate** on it. Rate limiting was sync-level only, and each sync constructed its own limiter at `load()` time, so N parallel syncs sharing one endpoint got N independent buckets — the pacing held per sync and silently multiplied per portal. Two changes fix it. **`rate_limit:` is now settable on a destination block** (all 19 HTTP destination configs), mirroring the `retry:` override from [#277](https://github.com/drt-hub/drt/issues/277); precedence is **`destination.rate_limit` > `sync.rate_limit` > the 10/s default**, so a chatty CRM can be paced without slowing the Slack alert in the same project. And **limiters are now shared process-wide per endpoint**: a registry keyed by the *real quota holder* hands every sync touching one endpoint the same limiter, so four threads against one portal now pace through one bucket. The key is deliberately not `describe()` — that string collides every Slack webhook onto the literal `"webhook"` (unrelated workspaces would throttle each other) while *splitting* one HubSpot portal by `object_type` (the exact under-sharing being fixed). Instead each config declares its quota holder: the HubSpot portal (the token, never `object_type`), the Slack/Discord/Teams webhook, the Zendesk subdomain, the Airtable **base** rather than the table, the GitHub **token** rather than `owner/repo`, the Twilio account rather than the sending number, the REST API host rather than the path. Env-var *names* are preferred over resolved secrets in the key; an inline literal credential is **not derived from at all** — it collapses to a single constant, so two destinations of one type that both inline a literal share one bucket rather than getting one each. That errs *toward* throttling (a slower sync, never a 429), and naming an env var per account restores a bucket per account; the connector docs say so where it matters. A digest was rejected: CodeQL flags any fast hash of a credential-shaped value on the dataflow, not the algorithm, and a slow KDF has no place on a hot path for what is only a dict key.
